2090. ELIZABETH JANE WALES, alias Partridge, was indicted for stealing, on the 5th of August, at Greenwich, 2 sheets, value 10 s.; 1 blanket, value 4 s.; and 2 pillow-cases, value 1 s.; the goods of Thomas Stephens.—2nd COUNT, stating them to belong to Sarah Stephens.
SARAH STEPHENS . I am the wife of Thomas Stephens, a labourer, and live at No. 3, Rome-street, Greenwich. These articles are my husband's—the prisoner came to lodge at my house in July, and staid not quite a fortnight—these articles were let to her with the room—she took the key away when she left—I sent for a constable to open the room, and missed the things—I afterwards received a parcel containing the key and some duplicates, by which I recovered the property—I don't know the handwriting of the prisoner.
Prisoner. She shut her door upon me. Witness. I did so a week before, at half-past eleven o'clock at night, and she never came again that I know of—I don't know that she came that night, as I went to bed.
CHARLES NICHOLLS . I am a policeman. I apprehended the prisoner on the 5th of September, in East-lane, Greenwich—I told her it was for robbing her lodgings—she said she thought that was settled before that time—as we came along, she said she had taken the things and pawned them, except the blanket, which she gave a person to pawn for some drink—I found nine duplicates on her, but not of this property.
HENRY RYDER . I am shopman to Mr. Seaward, a pawnbroker. I have some sheets and the blanket, which were pledged—I have no knowledge of the prisoner—I took in part of the things—they were pawned by a female—the duplicates of them are in this letter—I have the counterparts.
Prisoner's Defence. I had parted from my husband, and was in great distress. I took her room at 3 s. 6 d. a week—on Monday I did not pay my rent—a friend called on Tuesdasy, and she asked him for it—I said she might allow a person to come up stairs without insulting them, and next morning she told me to get another lodging—I told my brother-in-law I had pawned some of the things, and had not the money to redeem them—he came on Saturday, and said he had found me a room at 1 s. 6 d. a week, and he would compromise things with the landlady, as he saw I was sorry for what I had done—he told me on Sunday he had agreed to see it paid—I wrote to her on Tuesday to give her a week's rent, instead of stopping in the room—my brother-in-law said she had a bill against me of 16 s. 8 d.; and he had said he would pay it—the following week he paid her 2 s.—she agreed with him to make it a debt, and take it of him as fast as he could get it—Mr. Marshall, my landlord, offered her 3 s. 6 d.
MRS. STEPHENS. Her brother-in-law called and paid 2 s. on the rent after she left, and brought me the key and a letter, with the duplicates—he said he would see the rent paid, if God spared his life—he has brought me 1 s. at a time, and he had a misfortune, or I should have received more—he has paid 5 s.—7 s. was due for rent—the things are pawned for 9 s. 6 d.—he never said any thing about paying the money advanced on the things—I don't recollect being offered 3 s. 6 d.—I will not say it was not offered;
but I was not willing to receive any money on the duplicates without having the whole—the prisoner offered me 2 s. the day she was taken, and I refused it—I gave her three weeks' lenity after I told her my intention, and they never came forward—she is a stay-stitcher, and would be a long time earning 9 s. 6 d.
GUILTY. Aged 39.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined Ten Days.
Second Jury, before Mr. Sergenat Arabin.
[trialAccount t18350921-2091]
2091. MARY ANN FINCH was indicted for a misdemeanour.
MESSRS. SCARLETT and ELLIS conducted the Prosecution.
GEORGE KING . I am a coach-driver. I took the prisoner and two others on the 25th of August from the Bricklayers' Arms to the Broadway, Deptford—the fare was 1 s. 6 d. each—the prisoner took a bag out of her bosom, and handed three sixpences to Ann Brown, who gave them to me—two out of the three were bad, the third was good—I found the two were bad by tendering them at the corn-chandler's—I am sure they are the same—I gave notice, and went with the policeman to find the prisoner and the other persons—they were nearly at Dentford Bridge—I saw them taken—they went to the station-house, and on the way, the prisoner threw herself on the pavement, near Greenwich turnpike—the officer took a bag out of her hand, which contained twenty-two sixpences.
Prisoner. I did not fall down at all. Witness. Yes she did, at the turnpike.
WILLIAM SMITH . I am a police-sergeant. I went with the last witness, and took the prisoner and two othes—the prisoner that I took, which was Brown, flung herself down—I found nothing on her but 2 s. 6 d., which were good, and some halfpence—these are the two sixpences that the coachman offered at the corn-chandler's—this basket was found left in the coach—It contains a file and some dirt—the prisoner owned it the next day.
GEORGE HARRIS . I am a policeman. I took this prisoner into custody—I saw something in her right hand—I said, "What have you got?"—she said money—there was 2 s. in sixpences good—In going over the brigdge she was very busy putting her hand into her bosom—she threw herself down—I seized her hand, and found in this rag twenty-two bad sixpences—a good half-crown, and some halfpence, were loose in her pocket.
JOHN FIELD . I am inspector of counterfeit coin to the mint. These twenty-two are all bad, and these other two—they are all cast in the same mould. Prisoner's Defence. I picked them up at the public-house door—I went to inquire about my husband, who was gone to Portsmouth—I was making the best of my way there with a woman—we went to a public-house to have a pint of beer—It poured with rain—a gentleman called my little dog out—I went to call him back, and saw this rag with the money in it—I put them among my own good money—they were all mixed together—I did not know that they were bad—I then went on to Deptford, and said, as it rained, I could not go further that night.
GUILTY . Aged 18.— Confined Eighteen Months.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.

2094. MARY WELLS was indicted for stealing, on the 30th of August, 1 sheet, value 3 s., 6 s., the goods of Matthew Moriarty, her master.
MARY MORIARTY . I am the wife of Matthew Moriarty, we live in the stable-yard at Greenwich. The prisoner was my servant—she worked about the house, and slept there—she did not pay for her lodging—I missed some bed-clothes—here is one sheet, that I know is mine by my work on it—and here is one which is the fellow to it.
Prisoner. I never was her servant—I lodged in her house—I was coming down the street to get a lodging in Deptford, and she said I stole the sheet. Witness. She was my servant for two months—I gave her victuals and lodging.
THOMAS M'GILL ( police-sergeant R 19.) I took the prisoner for stealing a sheet and two blankets—I found this sheet at Mrs. Gerrard's.
MRS. GERRARD. I bought the sheet on the 30th of August of the prisoner, I believe—for 1 s. 6 d.
Prisoner. I am certain she never bought it of me—when the officer took me to her house she said I was not the person. Witness. I mean to swear she was the person.
COURT. Q. Did you never in presence of the officer express any doubt on ths subject? A. When the officer brought her to me, she looked different—I did not own to the officer that she was the person—I did express a boubt at that time—she was not dressed in the same manner—when I bought it, the person had a dirty cap on, and a dirty face; and when the officer brought her to me she had a clean cap on, and a clean face—I buy second-hand clothes.
Q. What makes you know her now, if you did not know her then? A. I was not obliged to own to the officer that she was the person—I mean to swear she was the person.
COURT to THOMAS M'GILL. Q. What did Gerrard say respecting the prisoner? A. She said she was rather dirtier in her appearance; but now that she had a clean cap on, she was not certain whether she was the person; but afterwards, before the Magistrate, upon a sound recollection, she said she was the person.
COURT to MRS. GERRARD. Q. Was any body with you when you bought it? A. No—I bought it in my shop, No. 4, Gale's-row, Greenwich—I do not buy every thing that is bought—I do not know what the sheet is worth.
NOT GUILTY .

2097. JOHN KEENAN was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of September, 1 candlestick, value 3 s., the goods of James Watson.
JAMES WATSON . I keep the White Horse public-house, at New Charlton, in Kent. On the 6th of September, about ten minutes before eleven o'clock at night, the prisoner and another came in—they called for some ale—they put down a sovereign, and paid for it—they then called for half-a-pint of gin—I saw they had had enough; and I told them, as soldiers, to keep sober, and go to their barracks—they went into my parlour, and drew the door to—when I would not draw them the liquor, away they went—I saw them go into the road—I then went into the parlour, and missed the candlestick—I had no suspicion that they had taken it till the next morning, when the watchman brought the candlestick to my house.
PETER INCH . I am a watchman. I was on duty at half-past eleven
o'clock, and saw the prisoner in Bowling-green-lane, talking to a woman—I saw he and this candlestick in his hand—I asked him where he got it—he said a woman gave it him, but he could not tell me who.
Prisoner's Defence. A woman gave it to me to h old—I waited, and she did not come back—I said to my comrade "I would throw it away"—he said, "Don't do that"—I then said I would give it to the first woman I met; and I was going to give it to that woman when the officer came up.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18350921-2098]
2098. THOMAS NEWBERRY was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of December, 1 time-piece, value 5 s., the goods of Robert Cummings.
ROBERT CUMMINGS. I am a gun-maker, and live in Artillery-place, Woolwich. I missed this time-piece on the 1st of december—I had not seen it perhaps for nine months before—I went to Mr. Davis, the pawnbroker, and there I found it—this is it—the prisoner was my servant.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. Had not other marines access to your house as well as the prisoner? A. Yes? some marines and many artillery men—I received information from John Ray Bailey, and then I missed this time-piece—I prosecuted Bailey, and he was transported for stealing a pistol.
COURT. Q. Was that pistol kept in the shop or up stairs? A. In the shop, and I had this time-peice in the shop till I broke the stand, and then I took it to the back parlour, which is my bed-room, and put it into a drawer—I have no up stairs—I have asked persons into that room to have a bit of dinner—Bailey or his friends may have been there.
JAMES CHANDLER . I am a shopman to Mr. Davis, a pawnbroker. I produce the time-piece—It was pledged by a marine, and then taken out, and pledged again by a woman in the name of Carpenter.
MARTHA CARPENTER . I am the wife of John Carpenter. I bought the duplicate of this time-piece of the prisoner for 1 s.—It had been pledged for 3 s., and the prisoner said it was worth 15 s.; but when I looked at it, it was not worth 3 s., and I told the prisoner to fetch me my money again—I know his countenance, and his speech.
Cross-examined Q. When was it you saw him? A. The last week in January—I never saw him till I bought the duplicate of him—I was questioned as to who I bought it of, when the prisoner was taken, which was last month—all marines are dressed alike—I had not seen the prisoner from the time he sold me the duplicate—I said he was the person, and his wife told me I bought it of him.
Q. Have you never said you could not tell? A. No; I said I had no doubt that was the man—I was not asked many questions about it—I did not know what I was going before the Magistrate for—I was never pressed but by the Magistrate and his wife.
COURT. Q. Did you not say it was a man very much resembling the prisoner? A. Yes; I said so to the Magistrate.
NOT GUILTY .

[trialAccount t18350921-2106]
2106. GEORGE JOHNSON was indicted for stealing, on the 25th of August, 1 pair of stocking, value 2 s.; 1 flannel shirt, value 6 d.; 1 pair of trowsers, value 6 d.; 3 shirts, value 6 s.; 2 waistcoats, value 3 s.; 1 jacket, value 1 s.; 1 handkerchief, value 6 d.; and 1 printed book, value 6 s.; the goods of James Bentley, in a vessel on the navigable river Thames.
JAMES BENTLEY . I was seaman on board the Gerorge and Eleantor, lying off Rotherithe. On the night of the 25th of August, I was called by Campion to a man who was said to be in the forecastele—I took hold of the prisoner—I missed from my chest some shirts, and my Bible—I found my Bible outside my chest, with these other articles—the prisoner was alonside of them—he had no business in the vessel—he told me he had been three hours in the forecastle, asleep—this was at eleven o'clock—some of the articles had been taken from my bag, and part from my chest—the prisoner told me that he was going to do nothing with the things, and that he had been asleep three hours in the foreccastle—he gave no further account of himself—my chest was in the forecastle.
Prisoner. He said before the Magistrate that he did not know whether he had put the Bible in the chest or not. Witness. I had put it in my chest—I said I put it in overnight in the chest.
THOMAS MOODY . I am a Thames police-surveyor. On the 25th of August, I went on board the George and Eleanor, and took charge of the prisoner—I found some clothes lying close to him—he was siting down in the forecastle, with neither hat nor shoes om—I asked him how he came on board—he said, "In a boat, from Union-stairs"—I asked what had become of his shoes—he said he did not know—I asked him if there was any body with him in the boat—he would not make any answer to that.
Prisoner. I was sitting by the stairs, about nine o'clock—I asked a person for a passage—as I sat on the stairs as young man came up, and asked me if I had not got a ship—I said no; and that I belonged to Aberdeen—he said he belonged to Edinburgh—he said, "I have got a ship, you can come on board with me"—he took me down to Union-stairs, gave me two glasses of rum, and then took me on board the vessel—he told me to take off my shoes, for if the men found me in the ship, he might lose his berth—I was not to tell how I came on board—I took off my shoes, and went down—the Bible laid on the chest—I laid down to sleep, and the young man whispered to me to go behind, as he wanted to get something out of the chest, and then the prosecutor came—I was sitting on the chest when he took hold of me—I was not by the clothes, and had not touched them—when the prosecutor went on deck, he said her saw a young man. and one of the boats was gone.
JAMES BENTLEY re-examined. I did not say I saw another person there—these are my trowsers—all these things were either in my bag or chest, all safe—all the ship's company were in bed—I asked him if there was any body else there, he said no—his cap was in amongst the clothes—the vessel was lying in Rotherhithe tier, on the Thames—the best of the things were put into the bag—they laid on the floor of the vessel as if they had been just taken out—nobody but him, could have taken them—he was a stranger—we were about two hundred yards from shore—I do not know how he got on board—he came on board in the night—he could not have come from one vessed to another, as there was a watch in the vessel alongside—I had been in bed about two hours.
Prisoner. A young man took me on board—I did not know but he
belonged to the ship—Bentley said he saw the man, and he said he found my cap on deck; now he says it was in a jacket.
JAMES BENTLEY . I found the cap in a man's jacket—I did not say I knew there was another man who had got away in a boat.
Prisoner. When he went on deck he called out police, and he said, "Oh! there is somebody with him, for the boat is gone. "
Witness. I said there was a boat under our bow, but I found it was our own long-boat—I did not say it was gone.
Prisoner's Defence. The young man who took there has got off, and I am left in the lurch—I was not found with any thing on me.
GUILTY . Aged 19. Transported for Seven Years.
First Jury, before Mr. Baron Gurney.
[trialAccount t18350921-2107]
2107. HENRY DEMPSEY was indicted , for that he, on the 2nd of September, at St. Mary's, Lambeth, feloniously did forge a certain receipt for money, to wit, for the sum of 5 l., with intent to defrand Sarah Eliza Massey, against the Statute, &c.—2nd COUNT, for feloniously offering and uttering the same, well knowing it to be forged, with a like intent,—3rd COUNT, that he having in his custody and possession a certain receipt for money, as follows, that is to say—" Received of Mr. Dempsey the sum of 5 l., for one quarter's rent, due the 25 th of March last,, 1835.—S. MASSEY," feloniously did alter the same, by then and there altering the figure 5 between the figure 2 and the word of into the figure 4, and by striking out and oblitersting the word "March," and writing above the same the word June, whereby it became, and is as follows (that is to say,) "Received of Mr. Dempsey the sum of 5 l., for one quarter's rent, due 24th of June last, 1835,—S. Massey"—with a like intent.—4th COUNT, for uttering the said forged receipt, with a like intent.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
SARAH ELIZA MASSEY . I am sinle, and live in Cornwall-road, Lambeth. The prisoner rents a house of me, at 20 l. a year, in Cornwall-road, next to where I live—I gave his son a receipt for the quater's rent, due in March—I cannot recollect the time—( looking at a receipt) this is the receipt I gave him, I believe; it now purports to be a receipt for a quarter's rent due on the 24th of June last—the 4 having been altered from a 5, and "March" struck out, and "June" written over it; but when I gave it to his son, it had the words "25th of March," and not the 24th of June—this alteration has been made without my consent or knowledge—I never received a farthing of the quarter's rent up to june—I wrote the whole of this receipt except the alteration—I have repeatedly applied to the prisoner since that for the June quater—he has put me off from time to time, saying I should have it in a fortnight, a week, and then ten days—I at last empolyed Cox to distrain for the June quarter, about the 2nd of September—I never saw this receipt except in the condition I gave it to him.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS Q. Are you married? A. No—"I sign my receipts, "Sarah Massey"—"the prisoner always paid me his rent by instalments of 1 l. or 2 l. at a time—"it may have been in June that I gave him this receipt—"I gave it him as a receipt for the last quarter—"he had it of me after the 24th of June, because he then completed paying for his June quarter—"he always said one quarter after another was very good payment—"I went seveal times to demand the rent—"this receipt is not on
stamp—henever showed me this receipt when I asked for the June rent—when I sent the broker in, the prisoner produced this receipt—I snatched at it, and tore a bit off the corner, as the broker said he had produced a receipt for the rent—I said it was not possible, and went to him—he had shown it to the broker—the word "March" is visible in the receipt now, but it has been scratched over with a pen—I do not recollect saying at the police-office, that I was ready to make it up, provided the prisoner paid me—I might have said so.
MARY ANN TYLER . I am a widow, and live in Cornwall-road. I know the prosecutrix and prisoner—I have applied to him on her behalf for the quarter's rent, due in June last—I applied for it within about tea days before this receipt was produced at the office—he said he had no money at all then, but as soon as he had, Miss Massey should have some—he never said he had paid it.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. In what month was this conversation with him? A. In June, at the latter end—later than the 20th.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. It was not due till the 24th? A. No; it was after it was due that I applied—I think it was more than a month after it was due—I am sure my application was for the rent due last June.
SAMUEL COX . I am a broker, and live in Tower-street, Westminster-road. On the 2nd of September, I went to levy a distress on the prisoner's goods, at the suit of Miss Massey—I demanded a quarter's rent, due on the 24th of June—I saw the prisoner—he said he owed nothing; that he had paid the rent, and got a receipt—I asked him to show it to me—he went up stairs, and brought down this receipt, and produced it to me, altered as it is now, from March to June, and 25 to 24—Miss Massey came in, and it was shown to her—I was outside the door when she snatched at it.
JOSHUA DELLER . I am an officer of Union Hall. On Saturday, the 5th of September, I went with another constable to the prisoner's house, and told him I had come for a quarter's rent due to Miss Massey—I did not state what quarter—he said he did not owe any rent, that he had paid it, and had a receipt to show—I said I should be satisfied if he produced the receipt to me—he went up stairs and brought down this receipt in his pocket—he put his hand in his pocket, and said, "You look like two gentlemen; I don't know you, and shall not allow it to go out of my possession, as it has once before been snatched at in my hand"—he held it in his hand for us to read—my brother officer asked him for it—he said he should not deliver it—my brother officer said. "We are officers of?Union Hall, and have a warrant against you, and have come for that receipt"—he said, "You shan't have it"—my brother officer laid hold of him, and the prisoner called to his wife to take it away from him—I pushed her away, and succeeded in getting it from him—he called, "Murder," and "Thieves" ( receipt read.)
(Enoch Lloyd, builder, St. James's-place, Cornwall-road; Thomas Bebys, cheesemonger, Cornwall-road; James Philips, printer, at the Morning Herald office; Benjamin Hardy, plumber, Cornwall-road; Hannah Harris, little Windmill-street, New-cut; and James Lake, plasterer, George-street, Blackfriars-road, deposed to the prisoner's good character.).
GUILTY of uttering only. — Confined Two Years.
Before Mr. Justice Williams.
[trialAccount t18350921-2108]
2108. JOHN SEAL was indicted for stealing, on the 21st of September in the parish of St. Giles, Camberwell, 1 bull, price 10 l., the property of Daniel Lewis.
ABRAHAM UPSDELL . I am servant to Daniel Lewis, a farmer at Camberwell. He had a bull, which I put into the field on Sunday afternoon, September 20th, at three o'clock—there were eight cows and a heifer in the field with him—I have had him seven or eight months—I locked the gate of the field and had the key in my pocket—I went to the field about half-past five o'clock on Monday morning—the cows and heifer were there, but not the bull—he had strayed away into the next field before—I saw the track of one oxen out of the gate, which was shut, but the lock was stretched as wide as it could—the gate had not been open—I saw the bull again on Tuesday.
DANIEL LEWIS . I am a farmer, and live in Camberwell parish. I was disturbed by the bull roaring before three o'clock on Monday morning—he bellowed as bulls do when after cows, and kept roaring all the way along the road—I judged it to be my bull's voice—I dressed myself, came down stairs, took a candle and lantern, and went on the common in front to see, if I could, which way it had gone—while I was looking about, a person from Peckham-rye came up—I followed to Walworth-terrace, about two miles and a half from my place towards London, and came up to the bull at the terrace in about an hour—I found the prisoner driving it—he had no other cattle—the bull was not making a noise when I came up—I asked the prisoner where he was going with that bull—he said to Smithfield, to Mr. Gurney, a salesman, and he had brought him from Lordship-lane-hill—I asked who employed him to drive it—he said "Mr. Kemp"—I told him it was my bull, and I should detain him—he said could I swear to it?—I said yes—I could recognise its head, by the colour of its horns, by the light of the lamp; and when daylight came I knew it well to be mine.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. After saying he was going to Gurney's, did he say any thing more? A. He said he was to have 3 s. 6 d. for driving it to market, and wished to be taken to Mr. Gurney to have the matter explained—the police took him to the station-house—I have had the bull about seven months, and was in the habit of seeing it myself once or twice a-day—I am quite sure it is mine—It had a very small head and particularly white stripes, and a white helly—I could distinguish its voice.
Prisoner's Defence. I was employed by Mr. Kemp to take it to Mr. Gurney, and was to have 3 s. 6 d. Four o'clock is the usual time to get to Smithfield.
GUILTY . Aged 48.— Transported for Life.
Before Mr. Baron Gurney.

2109. JOHN DEAN, JOHN SMITH , and JOHN RUSSELL , were indicted for burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of William Driver, at St. Mary, Lambeth, on the 13th of September, and stealing therein two dishes, value 1 s. 6 d.; and 1 pie, value 1 s.; his property.—2nd COUNT, stating it to be the dwelling-house of John Kershaw.
WILLIAM DRIVER . I am a policeman, and live at Belvidere-wharf, in the parish of St. Mary, Lambeth. I have the care of the wharf—It is not my house—I live in it rent-free till it is let—It is Mr. John Kershaw's house—last Sunday week I week on duty at half-past five o'clock, and when I came back I found the house had been robbed—I missed a dish, containing a plum-pie, and a dish which covered it—they
had been standing in the counting-house on a large desk under the window—I saw them safe at half-past five o'clock—the counting-house is part of the dwelling-house—the window was shut when I went out, and the blind drawn down before it.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. When did you see the window? A. When I went out—Mr. Kershaw applied to my Superintendant for a pliceman to go and take care of the house on account of the numerous depredations committed there—I have lived there nearly eighteen months, and have no other residence—Mr. Kershaw, I believe, has not lived then for the last four or five years.
SERJEANT PYE DRIVER . I am brother of the last witness, and was living with him at the time. On the Sunday morning, about six o'clock, I was getting up, and saw the three prisoners go from the window of the counting-room—Smith had got the pie in his hand—he was not a yard from the window—they all went away together—they went up Sutton-street—he there put the pie into a yellow handkerchief—they all went away together—I came down and went after them, but did not catch them—I found the window open, and a blue and white dish outside the window, which had been inside—the cash was up quite sufficient for a person to get in, and the blind shoved on one side—I am quite sure the prisonera are the person I saw—on the Monday evening, about eight o'clock, I saw Smith and Dean together in astreet ner the station-house—I pointed them out, and gave them in charge to a policeman—a handkerchief was produced at the station-house, in Smith's presence, which I believe to be the handkerchief he put the pie into—there was the print of an oval dish on it, and the pie was in an oval dish.
Cross-examined Q. Were thier backs or faces turned to you? A. Smith and another had their faces to the window when I first saw them—the street they went up is about hundred yards from where I first saw them—I had them in sight all the way up the street—I saw their backs and faces several times going up the street—my window was partly open—I looked through the window—through the glass—I saw the handkerchief put round the pie when they were abount a hundred yards off—It was such a handkerchief as the one produced—I cannot swear to it.
WILLIAM ALIDERMAN . I am apoliceman. I was in the New-cut on the Sunday, at about twenty minutes before seven o'clock, about a quarter of a mile from Belvidere-wharf—I saw the three prisoners together—I went and apprehended Smith the next night—I found this handkerchief on him—It has an oval mark on it now—I told him I wanted him for stealing a pie from the Belvidere-road, at six o'clock on Sunday morning—he said he had not beeen on that side of the water that morning, and afterwards he said he had not been there so carly as seven o'clock—I had not then told him the time the robbery was committed—It had never been named.
Cross-examined Q. Did you see the pie at any time? A. No—I had not told Smith that I had seen him before seven o'clock that morning—Dean was with him—I went up to them and told them, I took them for stealing a pie in the Belvidere-road—they were about five minutes' walk from the premises when I saw them at twenty minutes before seven o'clock.
WILLIAM DRIVER, JUN . I was in the room, with my uncle, on Sunday morning and saw the three prisoners below—Smith had a pie in his hand, and was about a yard from the counting-house window—they went away; and about a hundred yards up Sutton-street he put the pie into a yellow
handkerchief with white spots—he covered it up, and went away—I am sure the prisoners are the persons.
Cross-examined Q. Had you ever seen them before? A. I had not—I saw the handkerchief under the window, in Smith's hand—I made haste to dress as quick as I could, and went after them, but could not see them—I was or three minutes dressing—I went up Sutton-street, and down York-road, bout could not see them—I saw them for about five minutes altogether—I saw all their faces and backs, too, distinctly—I will swear I told my father what I had seen.
GEORGE MABER . I am waiter at the Waterman's Arms, in Waterloomad. Of the Sunday morning, about half-past six o'clock, I was sweeping the door opposite Belvidere-wharf—I saw a dish standing outside the window—I heard of the robbery, and give it to Mrs. Driver.
HANNAH DRIVER . I am the wife of William Driver. I received a dish from the last witness—I had set it on the counter, under the window, about eighteen inches from the window, over night—this dish had covered the pie—I valued the dish and pie at 3 s.
JAMES NEWMAN . I am a policeman. I apprehended Russell on Tuesday morning, at Marsh-gate.
Dean. I wish to ask the boy, whether he was on the first or second floor.
WILLIAM DRIVER, JUN . I was on the second floor—the window was shut.
Dean's Defence. Is it likely, a person at a two-pair of stairs window could distinguish yellow from white?—I was living at Mr. Ford's at the time, and was at work there till half-past eleven o'clock on Saturday night—I went to bed, and got up about five minutes to six o'clock; acme over the water to George Coutr, and called on Smith, to go and deliver a letter—we called Russell up, and went from one house to the other drinking, till about eight o'clock then we went home.
Smith's Defence. It is not likely a lad, at an up-stairs window, could see a yard from the house, without the window being open—the witness he picked up the dish about twenty minutes to seven o'clock—the policeman says he saw it in the New-cut, at twenty minutes to seven o'clock—I know nothing about it, I assure you—It is not likely he could distinguish white spots in a yellow handkerchief a hunderchief a hundred yards off.
DEAN— GUILTY . Aged 22.
SMITH— GUILTY . Aged 21.
RUSSELL— GUILTY . Aged 19.
Transported for Life.
Third Jury, before Mr. Recorder.

2210. WILLIAM SHOWELL, GEORGE GROUT , and DANIEL HADLAND , were indicted for stealing, on the 25th of August, St. George the Martyr, Southwark, 1 mare, price 50 s. the property of Thomas Price.
THOMAS PRICE . On Monday, the 24th of August, I put my mare into a field in Bethnal-green, about eight o'clock, and missed her about half-past six o'clock the next morning—on the 26th I went to Romford market to inquire about it, and a little boy gave me information—I had put the mare in a field at Old Ford—there is a little gap at the corner of thye field where I led her in—there is a bank and a ditch round the field—It could not get out at the gap unless it were led out—It was blind—I found her in
Mr. Bennett's possession, on the 26th of August—his man had it locked up in his stable.
Cross-examined by MR. MAHON. Q. You placed it in the field? A. Yes; I led it through a gap—It could not walk out unless it was led—It was bilnd—It was a little bit of a gap as wide as this witness-box—there is a large pond by the gap—I had to lead her very carefully round it—It never strayed from me before—I have had it about three years—I never offered it for sale to any body—I could get 3 l. for it—It would fetch 50 s. at market—It was not lit for the knacker—I did not give it to any body to sell.
THOMAS FORTUNE . I am servant to Mr. Bennett, a butcher. On the morning of the 25th of August, I saw Hadland and Grout come down St. George's market, near tge Elephant and Castle, with this mare—Gront was riding it, and Hadland walking alongside of it—as they came down the market they saw me—Hadland called me by my name, and said, " Tommy, will you buy the pony?"—I knew him before very well—I said, "No; the pony will not suit me"—It was a very small pony, about the size of a donkey—I said, "No; it will not suit me; it is blind, and such an object I will not have it"—I called Mr. Bennett, My master, and said, "Here is a pony perhaps will suit one of your little boys"—my master truned round and saw it, and asked if I knew the two men—I said, "Yes; I know them both"—master bid 30 s. for it, and Grout took it—I saw him paid—my master gave them a bit of steak, and they went over the way and had sixpenny worth of gin—the pony was claimed the next day, and master gave it up to Price.
Cross-examined. Q. You knew both the men very well? A. Yes; I have known Handland six or seven years—I knew Grout by seeing him in Smithfield-market—I knew where Haldland lived—Showell came to the public-house while we were drinking the liquor, and master asked him wherebe got the pony—he said he bought it at the Elephant and Castle, and showed a paper with the name of the man he bought it of—I think it was somebody at Bromley, but I cannot read—my master reae it, and Showell had the paper back again—I think master gave 10 s. too much; for it is blind and had a great wen at the bottom of its belly—I never saw such an object of a pony for the money—It could walk about—I am not accustomed tohorses much—I have one myself—no doubt a blind pony could find its way out of a gap.
COURT. Q. You think if there were a small gap, the size of that box, a blind pony would find it out? A. Yes—In fact I had one myself and If I turned him into a field, and there was the least gap inthe world, he would find it, and ger out—he was blind.
SOLOMON BENNETT . On Tuesday, the 25th of August, I saw Hadland and Grout at my house—I did not see Showell then—they called my man by his name, " Tommy, will you buy a pony?"—he said, "No; it won't suit me," and he said to me, "Master, here is a pony will suit one of the children"—I was busy serving some customers, and said, "well if you think so, buy it"—he came to me and said they wanted 2 l.—I said, "Give them 30 s., if it's worth it"—they agreed for that, but said if I would give them something to derink, and a bit of steak, I should have it—I gave Grout the money, and a bit of steak and went across the road to a public-house and treated them with something to drink—Fortune said he knew where to find them at any time—In the mean time Showell came in—I said, "Have you any thing to do with this pony?"—he said yes, that they
had all three bought it just now at the Elephant and Castle—I said, "Do you know who you bought it of?—he said, "Yes," and showed me a paper with a name on it which I forget in Bromley-lane—the next day, a neigbour of mine went to Romford when he came home he told me he heard the pony inquired after there—I asked why he did not tell where it was, and I went and gave information to the police—I sent Fortune down to the Elephant and Castle to see if he could find the men—he returned, and said Grout wasthere—I took a policeman tohim, and he said he and Showell had bought it, and he took us to Showell's house, but he was not at home—we brought him to where the pony was, and the prosecuter came up—I was going to give it to him but thepoliceman made him describe it first, which he did, and I gave it to him—Grout eas taken to Union-hall and romanded till the next day—Showeel I believe, came to the office and was detained.
Cross-examined. Q. Had you given the full price for it? A. More than it would fetch again—Showell came into the public-house and said that he bought it at the Elephant and Castle—he produced a paper to me—he did not say what he paid for it, nor describe the man he bought it of—he said he knew him by sight—Fortune knew them, well and where they lived—It was between ten and eleven o'clock in the open day-time—showell came forward voluntarily.
Q. Were you present when he made a statment the next day? A. I was at the police-office—he gave the same account as he did to me—I live in a publice thoroughfare, St. George's Market—they did not ask me to go to a private place to but it.
THOMAS PRICE . I am certain the pony is the one I lost—the field is in old ford, about five miles from Bennett's I think.
HENRY BANKS . Price came to me and asked if I knew st. George's market—I put my pony into the cart, and drove him over to the market, where I made inquiry, and found Bennett talking to the polliceman—I said, I had come for the pony—he said "What sort of a one was it? "—I said, "A small one a mare nearly black with grey hairs and a big mane"—Taylor was alongside—I said, "I must give this gentleman into your custody—I asked Bennett to let me see the pony—he said, "No, allow me to go and get the person I bought it of"—he went and brought Showell and another not in custody—showell was detained.
Showell's Defence. I was at the Elephant and castle—a man came to me and said, "Have you any money? here is a pony we can go and buy"—I went round to the knacker's—the man asked 25 s. for it—I said, "It is not worth that"—he said the knacker had bid 18 s. for it—I at last agreed to give 19 s. for it—he gave me his direction—we went to a public-house and had a pint of beer—I had to pledge to get the money and while I was gone he wrote the direction.
MARIA GRISBY . My husband is a horse-dealer and knacker, and lives at no. 2, Walworth-road, at the tripe-shop. On Tuesday the 25th of August a man called at out place with a pony about elevan o'clock in the morning or I think half-past ten—my husband was not at home—It was a man about five feet seven inches high—an oldish-looking man, in a fustian caot black hat dark whiskers, and I think corderoy or fustian trowsers—he was rather lame with one leg—I did not notice which—he asked me if my husband was at home; I said not—he said, "I have brought a pony for sale how long do you think he will be before he is at home?"—I said, "About an hour"—he said, "I will go round to Jones's to have pint of
beer, and tie the pony at the back of your house;" which he did, and when my husband came home I told him of it—he tied the pony in our back yard—Jones is a publican—my husband returned in about an hour—I told him, and he went to him to Jones's, and said he should not buy it—It was a dark brown pony, eith a great many grey hairs about it—It was very old, and blind with both eyes—such a one as we would not give much for—It was very bad indeed in appearance.
Q. Does not your husband buy them for slaughtering as bad as that? A. Perhaps the price did not suit.
BENJAMIN GRISBY . I am the husband of the last witness, and keep a tripe-shop in Walworth-road—I buy the horses to slaughter—on the 25th of August, I went to the Waggon and Horses, public-house, in consequence of what my wife said—when I came home I found the pony tied to the back of my house—I asked her what sort of man it was—she said an elderly man in a fustian coat—I went and saw nobody in the public-house but him—It might be between ten and eleven o'clock—It was neither of the prisoners—he was fifty years old, and I think lame—I said, "Have you got a horse to sell?"—he said, "Yes"—I drank with him, as he had apint of porter—I went and saw the pony—he said it was worth 30 s.—I said, "No"—he said, "Will you give 25 s.?"—I said, "No, it did about the place for half an hour after that, I dare say, and the pony was still tied up—In about a quarter of an hour I saw him taking it away towards the Rockingham Arms, New Kent-road—I knew nothing of it being sold till the policeman came and asked if I was not about buying a pony on Tuesday; I went to Union Hall, I there saw the same pony—I have not seen the man since—I should know him if I did—I never saw either of the prisoners near him—the Elephant and Castle is a place where ponies are often exposed for sale by men out of the country—I have bought many myself and seen hundreds bought.
Prisoner's Defence. I had 1 s. in my pocket when I bought it—I could only pawn for ten more, and when I came back I could only give him 11 s.—I said, "I had not enough, will it make any difference if I give you the rest to-morrow?"—he said, "Not at all, but you must give me my cloth and saddle back"—we had sixpenny-worth more gin—In about half an hour he brought me a halter—I was to give him the money and halter the next day—I told him any body about the elephant and Castle would tell him where I was if he inquired—I went over the water, and when I came back, I heard of the disturbance—I went to the policeman, and said, "I would meet him next morning, as I understood I was wanted—I went according to promise, but he did not come—a man was apprehended last Saturday, but it did not prove to be the man—Brown, the policeman, was with me two days looking for the man.
THOMAS SPENCER . I am a hackney-coachman. On the 25th of August, a person in a fustian-coat, and fustian or corderoy trowsers, spoke to me about Showell—I had seen the man on Tuesday standing with a pony against the cat's-meat shop door, kept by Grisby, in Walworth-road—It was the same man who spoke to me on Wednesday, and asked for Showell—It was an oldish looking pony, blind, and lame, and had a lump right undserneath its belly—the man in the fustian jac ket had a kind of dent in his nose—I could point him out from a thousand.
HENRY BANKS re-examined. Grout was produced to me with another man—neither of the prisoners—It was not a man answering the deescription of the
man alluded to—the man who is not here, and myself, and Taylor, took one day to search for the man they described—two men were brought to the Magistrate by Bennett, and the Magistrate told them they were all four guilty alike.
SOLOMON BENNETT re-examined. I produced nobody but Grout—there were four men before the Magistrate—they gave evidence, but the fouth man was never in custody.
HENRY BANKS . He was produced by you as a prisoner.
Witness. No such thing; Hadland came as a witness for Grout—there might have been a fourth man before the Magistrate, but I did not produce him—I believe it was the prisoners produced the fourth man—I think his name was Chetham, but I had never seen him—the Magistrate did not detain him.
MR. MAHON. Q. Did you hear the Magistrate say they were all guilty alike? A. Yes; the three prisoners, and the man not here—Hadland came as a witness, and Showell came afterwards, voluntarily—Showell and Grout have been out on bali ever since.
THOMAS FORTUNE . I have known the prisoners four or five years—Showell and Grout's parents keep cows, and they are cow-keepers—I have seen them about the Elephant and Castle buying horses, and any thing of that sort—old cart-horses come by in waggons.
COURT. Q. Do you mean the Elephant and Castle, where coaches stop? A. Yes; I have seen horses sold at Davis's door, who keeps a knacker-shop in the neighbourhood—Showell's father is a cow-keeper at Walworth—Hadland's brother keeps cows—I knew where to find Showell and Grout—It is down the first street on the left hand, in the Kent-road, close against the Elephant and Castle—I have seen them buy a horse, and sell it at Smithfield—I do not know any other way that they get their living—I am at Smithfield every day of my life, and have seen Showell there buying a horse—he produced a paper to me and my master.
HENRY BANKS . I had the direction in my room three days, to ascertain if I could find the man—Taylor the policeman has got it—he is not here.
Prisoner Showell. I gave the paper to Taylor—I have bought two and three horses a day, and sold them at Smithfield—a man may often buy three horses a day, without a halfpenny in his pocket, if he sells one as fast as he buys it—I have used every exertion to find the man.
SOLOMON BENNETT . They came to me between ten and eleven o'clock in the morning. I took the policeman to the Elephant and Castle, to take Grout—I said to Grout, "You know who you bought the pony of"—he said "Yes," and took me to Showell's house—Grisby's is about five or six hundred yards from my house—I brought it at very near eleven o'clock—It could not be later.
MARIA GRISBY . The pony was brought to my husband's about half-past ten or eleven o'clock.
BENJAMIN GRISBY . I went to the public-house, between ten and eleven o'clock, about the pony—I did not take the particular notice of the time—I parted with the old man about eleven o'clock—the pony had been standing there a good time—It was there some time after I spoke to him—I knew nothing about Bennett's buying it, till a policeman came, and asked if I had seen it—I do not think I was above five minutes with the old man—I was in the shop doing my business, and left him at the back—there ia a court behind my shop—he came round to the shop, and said,
"Won't you it of me?"—I said "No; it is not worth the trouble of skinning; it is so little. "
THOMAS NOYES . I keep a retail beer shop, London-road, opposite the Elephant and Castle. I have known Showell from a boy—on the Wednesday, a man about sixty years of age came to me, and inquired for showell—he was about five feet six or seven inches—he had one shoulder higher than the other, and was lame—It was on Wednesday the 26th—he staid at my house from haalf-past nine o'clock in the morning till half-past three o'clock in the afternoon—It was not either of the prisoners.
THOMAS SPENCER . On Tuesday morning, a man in a fustian coat, inquired after Showell, and on Wednesday I saw him at the Catherine Wheel—he asked if I had seen Showell or Stowell, he did not rightyly know the name—he did not say what he wanted.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Baron Gurney.
[trialAccount t18350921-2111]
2111. JOHN JONES , MARY ( the wife of William) JONES , and JAMES GLADWIN , were indicted, for that they, on the 11th of September, falsely made and counterfeited 3 pieces of counterfeit coin, resembling, and apparently intended to resemble half-crowns.
MR. ELLIS conducted the Prosecution.
JAMES FOGG . I am a Thames-police officer. On the 11th of September, I was at the prisoner Jones's hoiuse, in Propect-place, East-lane, Walworth, Mitchell, Isbester, and two other officers were with me—It was about half-past four o'clock in the afternoon—Mitchell went to the front door; I went to the back door; and when I heard a knock at the front door, which we had agreed upon, I opened the back door, went in, and found the three prisoners in the lower room—the door was on the latch—I saw the female prisoner throw something on the fire, and put the kettle on immediately, and run out at the back door, but she was afterwards bought back—Jones was sitting with his back to the window, close to the fire, and a table on the left-hand side of him—I ran to the table, and saw a counterfeit half-crown on it—there was a fine clear fire—I took up the half-crown on the table, and it was quite hot—I secured his left hand, and it was full of half-crowns—at that time Mitchellk had got in at the frount door—I gave Mitchell the half-crowns, and some of them were warm—I then secured his right hand, and from that I took nineteen half-crowns, which were not warm—I handcuffed him, and gave him to one of the officers to take care of—I went toward the fire, and Mitchell called me, and said, "Look here, Fogg, before I disturb this—there was half-a-crown mould in the fire, and the impression on it—It is plaster of Paris—It was the woman side of the impression—the reverse side—after that Mitchell took them out of the fire, and put them on the hob—while he was blowing his fingers, as it was hot, Jones put his foot upon the hob, and smashed the mould—I then Left Mitchell, and went to a chest of drawers in the room—I there found a stocking with several counterfeit half-crowns in it, which I gave to Mitchell—both the Jones' hands were black—such a blackness as would result from handling coin—I produce nineteen of the half-crowns, which I put in my pocket—I took them out of Jones's hands.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. If I understand you rightly, the three prisoners were in the room? A. Yes; I think Gladwin was sitting down in the room—he had his hat and a great coat on.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Had you reason to know that
the two Jones' were man and wife? A. No; I have not made inquiries about them—I do not know whether the officers of the Mint have—there are two rooms in the house—six of us went there—I think there were not seven—I and Mitchell had been at the Masons's Arms public-house before we went there—the other four officers were at another house in the Walworth-road—there was another person with us at the public-house—he went on his own business—he is a gentleman—I do not think I have a right to answer what he is; he is a respectable tradesman.
WILLIAM ISBESTER . I am in the Thames police. I was with the last witness on the occasion—I went first to a house nearly opposite the prisoner's and I saw from the first floor landing window, the prisoner, John Jones, come to the window with his coat off, his shirt sleeves tucked up, and holding in his right hand, something white, which I believe to be a mould—he went away for a few moments, and returned again, holding something in his hand, which I believe to be a whole mould—he was looking towards the end of it, the top—the female prisoner was in the room at the same time, sitting in the window, doing something by the motion of her hand, but what I cannot say, as a curtain went across the window; she was without her gown—this was between eleven and twelve o'clock—and between twelve and one o'clock the same day I saw John Jones come out of the house—he was gone about ten minutes, and then returned with the prisoner Gladwin, they both went into the house; stopped in ten or fifteen minutes, and came out again—Gladwin, John Jones, and an old woman, a child, and the female prisoner all came out—Jones and Gladwin came out first, and the others followed and shut the door—I came out of the house where I was, and saw them part—Gladwin went up a street, and John Jones went a-head, and his wife followed him—this was all on Tuesday the 8th, between eleven and one o'clock.
Q. State what happened on the 11th? A. On the Friday following, I went with Fogg, Mitchell, and the other officer—I got over some palings, about half-past four in the afternoon, and went to the back door of the house with Fogg and another officer—Fogg stopped at the door a few moments, with his finger on the latch, and when he heard a knock at the front door—he opended the door—I followed and went through the room to the door—I unbolted the front door, which was bolted at the top, and let Mitchell in, and went into the room again with him—I have heard what Fogg has said, and agree with it, as far as I was present after the prisoners were secured, on the table next to where John Jones sat, I found a small file, and in a cupboard next to where he sat, on the lower shelf, I found two paper bags, containing, I believe plaster of Paris, and a small paper of what I call fine sand, (but I believe it turns out to be linseed meal, the female prisoner calls it linseed-meal,) and something else with it; and I found in the same cupboard two tin bands.
ALEXANDER MITCHELL . I am a Thames police-constable. I was with the party—Isbester was acting under my orders on the 8th—on the 11th I went to the front door—Isbester let me in—I saw the two prisoners Jones and Gladwin—Fogg had got hold of Jones's hand, and he handed me some counterfiet half-crowns, which I produce—after the prisoners were secured I went to the fire, took off a tea-kettle, and saw some parts of a mould on the fire—one of them lying at the back part, and the half of a mould with the reverse side of a half-crown impressed on it—before I disturbed it, I called Fogg to look at it—as I took it out of the fire, it broke, and with the tongs and my fingers I took all I could see out of the fire and placed it on the hob—my fingers were burnt; and while
I was blowing my fingers, John Jones came behind me—he made either a kick or a bolw with his foot on the hob, and smashed the pieces of the mould more than they were before—I afterwards received from Fogg six crowns and eight half-crowns in a stocking, they are counterfeit—I searched the ashes, and found an iron ladle, with the appearance of white lead in it—on the top of a cupboard or dresser I found the half of half-a-crown, and one metal spoon—on the table by Jones I found five other white spoons—I asked Jones who the house belonged to—he said "I took it;" and pointed to Gladwin, he said, "That old man has nothing to do with it—he knows nothing of my business, or what I do"—I afterwards took the moulds to Mr. Powell, the solocitor, and in my presence they put them together as they are now.
JOHN FIELD . I am inspector of coin to the Mint, and have been so nearly twenty years—I have examined all the nineteen half-crown produced by Fogg, they are all counterfeit—those nineteen produced by Mitchell are all counterfeit—and the six crowns produced by Mitchell are all counterfeit—the half-crowns are from two different moulds, of the date of 1821 and 1825, in the reigb of George the Fourth—here are eight more half-crowns, and a plaster of Paris mould; on one side of which here is part of the impression of the obverse side of a half-crown, and on the other, a very small portion of the reverse; of the reign of George the Fourth, 1825, which is the date of some of the half-crowns—this mould does not appear to me to have been used for castint—here is another plaster of Paris mould for half-crowns—that is broken also but there are portions of the impressions of the obverse and reverse sides of a half-crown of 1825; it has nearly the entire impression of the reverse side, this appears to have been used considerably, and I believe some of the half-crowns produced have been cast on this mould, for I find some corresponding indentures in the plaster which correspond with some of the half-crowns—here is another mouls for half-crowns also, which appears to be of the date of 1821, the figures are not complete, but here is the 2 and 1 and a portion of the 8, and the type corresponds with the impression of that year—here are two tin bands, such as are usually made use of for forming plaster of Paris moulds, and I believe these moulds have been formed in these bands—these white metal spoons are similar metal to the half-crowns, and that is the usual way coiners provide metal—here is plaster of Paris in powder, in the bag; and a file which is necessary to round off the edge of the coin—I do not know what this sand is used for.
MRS. KNIGHT. I am the wife of Richard Knight, who is owner of this house. It was taken from me in August by Jones and his wife—I do not know the woman to be his wife—Gladwin was with them, and seemed to think it sould suit them very well—he did not do any thing himself—Mrs. Jones always paid the rent.
Cross-examined. Q. How long have you known them? A. Since August—they appeared man and wife, and I thought they were so.
John Jones's Defence. I am entirely innocent of making bad money—I admit they were in my possession.
Mary Jones. I am the wife of John Jones. We were married at Carlow, seven years ago, on the 10th of last March.
JOHN JONES— GUILTY . Aged 30.— Transported for Life.
MARY JONES— NOT GUILTY .
GLADWIN— NOT GUILTY .
There was another indictment against the prisoners for having in their possession a mould on which was impressed the figure of a half-crown; on which no eveidence was offered.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Baron Gurney.

2112. JOHN HARDAWAY and JOHN BROWN were indicted for feloniously assaulting Frederick Temberman, on the 9th of September, with intent to rob him, and his goods and monies, from his person, and against his will, feloniously to steal.
FREDERICK TEMBERMAN . I am a cooper. On the 9th of September I had been drinking at a public-house with a friend—I got drunk, and in going home I lost my way, and met the prisoners, I cannot say where—they tripped me up and put something before my mouth like a handkerchief, and they were about my pockets—one got his hand into my pocket—I was on the ground—a policeman came up—I had never seen them before.
WILLIAM CUDMORE . I am a policman. At one o'clock in the morning of the 9th of September I was at the bottom of Pearl-row, South-street, Borough-road—I observed the prosecutor and two others—the prosecutor appeared drunk—they were all three together on the pavement—I knew the two prisoners, and as I went towards them I saw the prosecutor in the set of getting off the ground—he had been on the ground—the prisoners were standing there—he charged them with throwing him down, putting a handkerchief before his mouth, and putting their hands into his pocket—they said it was false, they had done no such thing, for he had fallen down—I directly took them both into custody, and after I had gone about three hundred yarda I met Piper, and he assisted me—the prosecutor collected himself in the interval, and laid hold of one of the prisoners by the neck of his coat—I found on Hardaway 1 s. 1 1/2 d., a handkerchief, and an old apron—and on Brown was found some money, and a false nose for the purpose of disguising his countenance.
Cross-examined by MR. JONES. Q. I believe they went very quitely with you? A. Brown did, Hardaway seemed to resist, but Brown persuaded him to go quietly—the prosecutor was drunk—he said he lived at No. 25, Pearl-row—I took him there, and found he had left there a month—I could do nothing but take him back to the station for safety.
Hardaway. When he took hold of me he tore the front of my shirt right out—I told him to take my collar, which he did, and I then went quietly. Witness. If I tore his shirt, it was in the struggle—I am not aware of it.
JAMES PIPER ( police-constable M 83.) I searched Brown at the station, and found 9 1/2 d. and this false nose on him, and some mustachios.
Cross-examined. Q. You found it in his pocket? A. No, in his hat.
FREDERICK TEMBERMAN Cross-examined. Q. Are you sober now? Yes—I cannot say when I was drunk last, but it might be last Wednesday—I have not been drinking since I attended the Grand Jury—I was fined last Wednesday evening, in going home, for being drunk—I cannot say where I had been the night before thos happened—I had not been drinking all night—I cannot say how long I had been drinking—I cannot say when I began—I had not been so tipsy for a good while as I was that night—I began drinking about ten o'clock in the morning—I cannot undertak to swear I had not quarrelled with one of the men before I was knocked down—I did not tumble down, for I was chucked down.
NOT GUILTY .
Fifth Jury, before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18350921-2113]
2113. WILLIAM MILLS, CHARLES COPPER , and WILLIAM SIMPKINS were indicted for a robbery on Moses Giddins, on the 20th of September, at St. Giles, Camberwell, patting him in fear, and taking from his person, and against his will, 2 sovereigns, 4 shillings, and 1 sixpence, the monies of the said Moses Giddins.
MOSES GIDDINS . On the night of the 20th of september I was at the Waterloo Arms public-house—I cannot say the time, but I do not think it had gone twelve o'clock—I am a milkman, and live at Camberwell—I had 2 l. 4 s. 6 d. in one pocket when I was in the Waterloo Arms, and 3 s. 6 d. left in another pocket when I came out—there were two sovereigns, four shillings, and a sixpence—Ellis was with me at the Waterloo Arms—I had not pot there, which was all I paid for—I had not been drinking any where else—I paid for one pot on my own account—I had drank with others—I do not know how much I drank—I was sober when I left the Waterloo Arms—I found myself in Waterloo-street and the three prisoners came up with several others—I am sure of the prisoners—when I got opposite them, Mills sung out, " Whitey! " (I had a white coat on)—I was going to make some observation; but before I could get the wards out of my mouth, I was knocked down by Mills—he hit me in the mouth with his fist—I had given him no provocation—when I was down I found his hand in my left hand waistcoat pocket; but I cannot swear he took my money—It had been in my left hand waistcoat pocket—Mills had his hand in my pocket, and Copper came and pitched down on my head—all his body came down on my head—he fell right across my face when I was down—I sung out for my brother to come and assist me—he was in the Waterloo Arms, and Ellis the gardener was round the corner—he heard me halloo out, and came before my brother did—Ellis came and pulled Copper off me; and when I got off, Simpkins was at me—he hit at me, but I do not hink he struck me—he hit at me in the row —that was after I called out—all three of them stood in front of me when I got up—when Ellis pulled Copper off my body, all three of them stood before me—Simpkins was with the other two when I was knocked down, but I did not see him do any thing—when I got up, they all hit at me—they did not strike me—when I got out of the crowd I pulled my jacket off, they were all three at me—Copper and Mills hit me—I never swore I knocked two of them down, and afterwards ran away—I could not—I shoved them off the gardener—what I stated before the Magistrate was read over to me, and I put my mark to it—I did not knock two of them down, that I know of—I shoved them down, and they were on the ground, but not with a blow, with a snove—I shoved them with my double fist—there is a difference between striking a man and shoving him—I pushed against them with my clenched fist—as soon as I missed my money I cut away to the station-house—I missed my money after they got off the ground—I put my hand into my pocket, and they were gone—that was the same pocket into which Mills had introduced his hand—I took Mills into custody—the policeman took him in bed—he told me he had not had my money—Mills said he would give me 1 l. on Monday morning, if I would let him go that night—he did not use the word "forgive. "
Q. If you have beer taken down to say that he said if you would forgive him, it is not true? A. If I would forgive him and let him go—If he had given me 1 l. that night, I should have forgiven him.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. You are what is called a fancy milk-man? A. No; I was never in a row before in my life—I never beat a man named Osaac Benham—I shall not
swear that—I shall not swear any thing about it—I do not think I have had a row with a man since I was at Camberwell—I have been at Camberwell seven months—I do not recollect beating a man named Benham six weeks ago—I cannot recollect whether I did—I will not swear I did not—I was not drunk—I do not know the man—I have not fought a man since I was at Camberwell—I do not think I fought with a man six weeks ago—( looking at Benham)—I do not recollect fighting that man, or quarrelling with him—I think I have seen him in my milk-walk—I did not beat him in my walk—very likely I had a row with him—I might have a quarrel with him—I think he is the man I saw in the Joiners' Arms, and I think there was a row there.
Fifth Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.
NOT GUILTY
[trialAccount t18350921-2114]
2114. JANE JACKSON was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of August, 2 pillows, value 8 s., the goods of Elizabeth Tucker.
ELIZABETH ISTEAD . I am the grand-daughter of Elizabeth Tucker. She lodges in my house—the prisoner was her servant—she left on the 5th of August, and I then missed two pillows of my grandmother's.
JOSEPH CLOTHIER . I am a pawnbroker. I produce a pillow pawned on the 1st of August, by Snell.
SARAH SNELL . On the 1st of August I asked the prisoner to lend me a shilling, as I wanted to get something done to take home—she said she could not, but she would go to her landlord and get a shilling, and she went and brought this pillow, which I pledged for two shillings.
Prisoner. That is the truth—I was in great distress—I had no where to go to, and no friend in the world.
GUILTY. Aged 28.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury and Prosecutor.
Confined One Month.

2115. CHARLES PARR was indicted for an assault on Mary Ann Parr, with intent to ravish her.
NOT GUILTY .
NEW COURT.— September 21 st, 22 nd, 23 rd, 24 th, and 25 th.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18350921-2116]
2116. THOMAS BOLTON, JAMES BARDELL, JOSEPH BARDELL, RICHARD BIRD, THOMAS BIRD, RICHARD CARPENTER, WILLIAM COWDEROY, JAMES HILL, JOSEPH JOHNSON, JOHN NEWELL, SAMUEL PEARCE, JOSIAH PARKES, PHILIP SELLICK, ROBERT TREVETT, THOMAS CORBETT, GEORGE DODD, WILLIAM GULLIVER, JOHN GOUGH, THOMAS TREVETT, EDMUND SYLVESTER, JOHN SYLVESTER , and JOHN SMITH were indicted for a conspiracy.
MR. SERGEANT BUMPUS, with MESSRS. WHITEHEAD, and BALL conducted the Prosecution.
AARON BRAY . I am a horse-dealer, and live in Red-lion-yard, Holborn. On the 8th of June I started two omnibuses, to run from Paddington, in Middlesex, to the Bank—I took out two licenses, Nos. 348 and 349—they ran from Paddington along Oxford-street and Holborn—at that time, Thomas Bolton, James Bardell, Joseph Bardell, Richard Bird, Thomas Bird, Richard Carpenter, William Cowderoy, James Hill, Joseph Johnson,
John Newell, Samuel Pearce, Josiah Parkes, philip sellick, and Robert Trevett had omnibuses on that road—my omnibuses started between nine and ten o'clock in the morning, from Paddington—on the first day, I was watching to see my omnibuses come down Holborn, and there were two or three omnibuses with each, hunting them, and driving them on as fast as they could; Trevett's and Bolton's omnibuses in particular, and Mr. Bird's also—I believe there was no other that day—I watched them several other days, and saw them drive close up to he pavement, and drive their poles close up to my omnibns, so that no passenger could get in—on those other days I saw the omnibuses belonging to the other defendants I have named doing so—I do not know that there was one who has not done so—the whole of them have been a continual annoyance—on the 15th, I lengthened one of my omnibuses to Maida—hill, still continuing on the same line of road, through Paddington—on that day, a new omnibus, called "The Emperor," No. 352, with the name of "Edmund Sylvester" on it, started against the one of mine, which ran to Maida—hill; and another, called "The Albion," No. 177, with the name of "Thomas Corbett" on it, started in opposition to the one of mine, which went to Paddington—John Sylvester drove the "Emperor, "and John Smith was the conductor; but whether they did so the first day it started, I cannot tell—Gough drove the "Albion," and one of the Trevett's, (Thomas, I think,) was the conductor—I had observed Cowderoy driving his omnibus the first week, which he every now and then does drive—he was one who ran his pole up against me, and put me on one side, and was annoying me—I saw the other defendants in the first week; and omnibuses would come down from Paddington to where I set out, wait till I came out of my yard, follow my omnibuses up to Paddington, wait till they turned round to go, and then immediately gallop close before them—one day, one of my horses came home with the wheel run over his foot—sometimes there was a pannel broken—I saw Sylvester's omnibus run against my pannel one day in Oxford-street—I have seen them come following my buses home, without plying for hire; and they would stop for an hour, to see whether we attempted to go out again—this was done frequently, every day almost—about three weeks after we started, there came a very rainy day—one of my buses let water—we took the passengers out, and put them in another bus —I got into the bus that let water, and made them drive it down to the maker's in Whitechapel; and the "Emperor" followed me all the way, and stopped there four hours—John Sylvester drove the "Emperor" there, and John Smith was the conductor.
Q. Did you ever meet any of the defendants any where? Q. Yes; the first I saw of them was Josiah Parkes—I forget when it was but it was near the beginning of my starting—I found him in my yard in Holborn, when I got there, we went into the public-house, and had a glass of brandy-and-water—he told me I was a madman to start if I did not buy times—that the "Paddington committee" would run me off the road, and ruin me—I said no, I should try—I alterwards saw Trevett, Hill, and Cowderoy, all three together, I think it was on the 4th of July—I had just come home from the bazaar, and got out of the bus, when Robert Trevett came, laid hold of my coat, and said I should go and have something to drink—I said I had not had my dinner, and I seldom took any thing before dinner—he said, would I come back—I said I would—I went back, and Trevett, Hill, and Cowderoy were there, drinking—they asked me whether I meant to do what was rights—I said I meant to stop if I could, and keep on my omnibus they said it
was no use, for their committee was so strong it was no use for one to oppose them—It would cost them about 10 s. a week each man, and it would cost me 20 l. to oppose them, and if I could afford to stand the loss of that I had better stop—I said, "Never mind that, I shall stop as long as I can"—Cowderoy said, "Why don't you buy times, and belong to us?"—I said I never would belong to any committee of that kind while I lived—I would stop, if I could, and Cowderoy said, "Ah, do, old one, start half a dozen, that will soon end it"—they got drinking very hard, and got warm with liquor, but nothing more passed about the omnibuses, except that I should be sure to be ruined; and Hill said, "He is an old one, and has been amongst us many years; we must let him in some how or another"—Cowderoy said, "Why don't you sell him your times?" and Hill said, "I will take five hundred guineas for them"—my son was there at that time—I left them to go home—Hill ran over the road, caught hold of me, and said they had been standing treat to me, and I must to them—I said I wanted no more, but I would give him a glass of brandy-and-water—we went, and Joseph Bardell came to the door—we went out, and spoke to him in the street—he said, "It is of no use your going against this committee; they will ruin you as sure as you are born; go and buy somebody's time"—he said they were such b----y out-and-outers, they would beat anybody, and even if Mr. Horne, or the greatest stage—master in London were to start against the Paddington committee, they would be sure to be beaten—he said, "Hill, why don't you sell him your times?"—Hill said, "I did offer them to him a little while age at five hundred guineas, but I won't take six hundred for them now"—my son was not present at that time—I had no further conversation with Bardell and Hill that night, but I have seen Bardell frequently—he told me they would be sure to best me—they would have nobody there but what belonged to the committee, and bought times—he said he belonged to the committee, but did not often go—Robert Trevett has called upon me three or four times—he has told me it was no use my running, they would suffer no one on the road except they purchased times—he said I had better purchase times, or I should be ruined—they had ruined several, and they would ruin me—Bolton has called upon me three or four times, and had the same conversation, but he advised me at one time to go to Kentish—town—I made a reply that I did not like Kentish—town, as the turnpikes were heavy—he said the turn-pikes should not cost me much, as he was proprietor of the gates—he said it was no use my attempting to go against so many of them, that they paid but a trific a week in, and there was such a body of them it was foolish of any one individual going against so many of them—I do not recollect having any conversation with any of the other defendants—I run on and was losing a great deal of money, till I called a sale at Mr. Allen's, in St. Martin's, on Wednesday, the 29th of July—my omnibuses stopped for that sale on Sunday night the 26th—the other omnibuses continued with me—they came dowm on Saturday morning, the 25th, and followed me all day long—they seldom came on a Sunday—I did not see them on the Monday—my sale took place on Wednesday—I sold five horses—my omnibuses were only on bire—on the Thursday my Paddington one went out again—the defendants' omnibuses were out that day, but I did not see them—on the Friday I saw the "Emperor" and the "Albion" follow my one bus —they tuco'd him, as they call it, all day, one before and the other behind—selveaterdrove the "Emperor," and I believe Gough drove the "Albion"—I started my other omnibus on the 2nd of August—the other omnibus still
continued to pursue me in the same way as before, or rather worse, as they supposed I was beaten, and they set too with more vengeance against me—one of my omnibuses is on still—the other ceased running on the 30th of August—the whole of this book is not in my handwriting, but I see it every day of my life, either night or morning—It contains the earnings of each bus—I have seen John Sylvester up at Paddington two or three times—he has said, "It is a pity you should go on so; why don't you come and buy time? they will never let you have any time without you come and buy time"—Smith joined in the same conversation—neither of them told me who employed them, but I have seen the same horses working in the "Emperor," and then in Mr. Bolton's omnibuses—and the horses worked in the Albion" in Mr. Trevett's—I have been a horse-dealer thirty-six or thirty-seven years, and have been unfortunate twice.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. What is your name? A. Aaron Bray; I am not a Jew—I was never in the omnibus trade till the 8th of June—I had been a hackney-coach and cab propritor—I put on two omnibuses—they had no name in particular. but my own name at full length on them—I employed James Raimes, John Lee, henry Mann, and Thomas Travett—I did not know that either of them had been in the service of Robert Trevett, nor do I know it now—they were recommaded to me by a person at Paddington as proper persons to drive my omnibuses—I have heard, since Thomas Trevett has left me, that he is a cousin of Robert Trevetts, and that they had had some words—but I did not hear that he had been in his employment—I knew there was a person of the name of Trevett on the road—I do not know that I ever heard that he had been Trevett's employ—I do not know what Raines had been before he came to my employ—I heard that he had been a pugilist—I have not heard that he had been concerned in a manslaughter case—I never went much into the ring —I was at a fight some years ago—I have been twice a bankrupt—the last time was the latter end of last year or the beginning of this—the first time was about seven years ago—I never compounded with my creditors, nor proposed to do so—I never gave my drivers any particular directions what to do—they asked me, when they came recommanded to me by Mr. Davidson, of Paddington, at what time they should come, and where they should go—I said, "Go where you like; I know nothing about the times; you know the business better than I do"—I never gave directions to Lee to keep close to Trevett, or to any one—they said so and so was a good time, giving me the hours, and I said very well—they said, "A quarter before ten and two are as good times as you can go;" and I said, "Go at those times"—I knew nothing about whose times they were—I never knew who went at seven, eight, nine, or any other hour.
Q. Did you ever tell Lee or Mann to stick close to Trevett's omnibus? A. Never in my life to stick close to any omnibus—I do not know Mr. Carr, a hatter, who keeps a shop opposite Day and Martin's—I do not recollect being at a hatter's shop opposite Day and martin's, on WhiteTuesday.
Q. Did you ever use this expression, that you had begun the opposition, and you would stick close to the old thief, Trevett, and take a little of the bounce out of him, let it cost what it would? A. Never in my life—I know budd, the timekeeper at the Bank—I never said in his presence that I would ran against the old cobler and melt his wax—Trevett taxed me with that—he said he had heard from his nepher that I had said those
words—I never gave my men orders to go in any times, nor to stick close to Trevett's omnibuses, because his times were good times.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. How long is it since your first bankruptcy? A. About seven years—I was then a horse-dealer, and in the same yard which I now keep—I purchased the premises about three months afterwards—they took bills for them at different dates—my first divident was 5 s.; there was another about 3 s. 6 d., and there will be another—I will swear that more than 5 s. has been paid—my second bankruptey was at the latter end of 1834—there has been no dividend declared on that as yet—It is in the hands of Mr. Green—I cannot tell what my creditors will get—Hill asked me to give 500 guineas to buy time—I dare say he knew I was a bankrupt—when a man is unfortunate it is generally known—I hava had conversation with a person of the name of Bardell, the brother of the two Bardells who are indicated—he is a vetrinary surgeon, and a very respectable man—he asked me how I came to indict them—I said it was my attorney's advice, and I could not go from it—my attorney is Mr. Norton—he advised me to indict the Bardells—I went with Mr. Bardell to Mr. Norton's, and there was a conversation about their putting in bail. Q. Now Mr. Bardell is alive, and is here; do you remember his asking Mr. Norton why he advised you to put his two brothers into the indictment, when they were innocent? A. I do not recollect it—Mr. Norton might have dropped such an expression as, That he was determined they should not be witness for the other defendants—It was not in my presence.
Q. Do you mean that such a villanous expression might be used, and you forget it; such an infamous expression? A. Mr. Norton's answer was, "we have no occasion to press the indictment against your brothers"—Mr. Bardell went on to say something about the bail, and Mr. Norton said, "The indictment has no occassion to be pressed against your brothers; they have not much to fear; but you must put in bail"—he said that after advising me to include them in the indictment.
Q. Was any thing said about a hundred pounds? A. Never to me, or to Mr. Bardell in my presence, upon my oath; nor did Mr. Norton say that he put the Bardells into the indictment that they should not be witnesses, nor anything to that effect—Mr. Norton did not tell me why he put the Bardells in the indictment—I went to him to indict the Paddington committee, and he indicted whom he pleased—I appointed with Mr. Bardell to meet him at Mr. Norton's—I went there to meet him—he got there first.
Q.
