Did Norton say that their names must remain in the indictment; his object was to prevent their being witnesses; because, if they were ommitted, the paddington proprietors would subpœna them against Bray? A. Never, sir; nor that their evidence would be of importance, I having commissioned Joseph Bardell to raise 100 l. to take my omnibuses iff the road, and discontinue further opposition; and if he could not get 100 l., to take 50 l., and they to take my stock at a valuation—there was not a word said about it—we were there half an hour, talking about the indictment a little, but the most was about putting in bail.
Q. Upon your oath. was not the appointment to go to Norton with Bardell to try to get his brothers out of the indictment? A. No; the agreement was for Mr. Norton not to oppose the bail, but take them on Monday—there was nothing said about getting his brothers out of the indictment—I never in any way wanted money to settle this business.
Q. Did Nonton say in Mr. Bardell's presence, that if the proprietors did not consent to your proposition, he would ruin Bolton, Pearee, Bird, and Trevett, or he would have his heart out and his limbs broken? A. No: Bardell was not desired to ask his brothers if the proprietors would come to terms—I saw Mann this morning—I do not know whether he is a witness for me—he is one of the persons who complained that he was hunted—I never gave Mann directions to drive against Bolton—not any person—I do not know this man ( looking at a man named Archer)—I never saw him before, to my knowledge.
Q. Did you ever say to your conductor, in Archer's presence, "Go on wwith that omnibus ( pointing to one), and stick to him; I will have the shirt off his back before I have done with him?" A. Never in my life—I know Parish, the policeman, No. 45—I never had any conversation with him about this—I do not know that ever he was obliged to lead my omnibus out of the way—I know No. 7 has—I never spoke to him in the Poultry about my opposition, to my knowledge—I know a person named Alexander—I have no recollection of mentioning the name of Alexander to Parish.
Q. Do you recollect his saying that you could not expect the people would let you take the bread out of their months withour giving you some trouble? A. No; I think I have heard such words, but I think it was from No. 7, not from Parish.
Q. Did you say, "I should have been very well off, if Alexander had not put me in the hold?" A. I said I should have been better off if Alexander had come out with me, as he promised—I possibly might say we should have made the Paddington masters look about them—I have been summoned two or three times by the police for causing an obstruction—I have heard of the names of Colson and Wilson as time-keepers—I have seen Colson, and spoken to him.
Q. Have you ever said this in his presence, That unless you could receive 100 l. from the proprietors, you would indict them all fro a conspiracy, and ruin them? A. Never in my life to any soul—I remember two of Trevett's mem being taken into custody.
Q. Do you remember using this expression, in the presence of Colson, that you had been as good as your word; it was not the men you wished to hurt, as you could get nothing out of them, but their masters, and that the law must take its course, unless you were paid 100 l. to take your omnibus off?" A. Never, to any man upon earth—I have spoken to Bates, the policeman, No. 7—he has summoned my driver two or three times fro obstructing the road and he summoned me twice, once was for taking time at the Bank—he said I should not take time—my man got off by paying 5 s.—I was summoned to Marlborough-street—one of Mr. Bolton's omnibuses ran against mine—my son got into a gallop, and they were both had up —It is the men that are summoned, but the master always attends, and if they are fined he pays it—I know Bates has often told me of my men doing wrong—I said, "Then summons them, I have nothing to do with it; I have spoken to my men about it, and they said they did no such thing"—I cannot tell whether Mann is here—I have not subpœnaed him—I was not afraid that he would tell the jury that I ordered him to drive against Bolton—I never said so—nor about getting 100 l.—I know the policeman, No. 13—I do not know his name—I recollect his having two persons in custody one night at the Mansion-house.
Q. Did you say on that occasion, this, or any thing like this—that you
would have all the omnibuses removed from the Bank; and you would be d----d if you did not line the street with buses, if it cost you five hundred pounds? A. Never—I mean distinctly to swear I never used to that man any such expression, but I remember his having the men very unlawfully taken up—their words were taken to come on Monday.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. About how many witnesses have been subpœnaed by Mr. Norton in this case? A. A great many—I have heard fifty or sixty—there were only four witnesses went before the Grand Jury and only two of them were heard—the amount of debts at my first bankruptey was about 5000 l. or 6000 l., and the second time not 1500 l.—I have not paid fifteen shillings in the pound on either commission.
Q. Are you aware that, not having paid 15 s., you are not in point of law, entitled to any property? A. No, Sir—I succeeded to my stable in about three months after my first commission—I never left it the second time—It was mortgaged to my bankers, and they let me remain.
Q. Was Thomas Bird a creditor under your second commission? A. Not that I know of—I do not know the man—I have no recollection of his name for a penny—I never got into his debt—I had some bills of exchange out, but very few—I have no recollection of Bird's name in either of my bankrupteies, or in any kind of dealing—I did not enter a bill of Bird's for a backney-coach—I have no recollection of it—I hired my omnibuses of Mr. Boswell, in Aldgate High-street—I set them up on the 8th of June—I paid part of the hire of them 17 l., having had them twelve weeks—I had my buses ordered before Alexander had any idea of coming out—he then told me he was coming out—I did not agree with him to bring out omnibuses.
Q. What did you mean by saying just now, if he had come out as he agreed? A. No, Sir, "as he said he would," I did expect him to come out with me, and I thought he was coming out on that day, but I heard afterwards the Paddington committee had given him 100 l., to stop him—I consulted with Mr. Norton a very little time after I started, I believe about three weeks—he had been my attorney before—I have heard that forty or fifty omnibuses ran from the Bank to Paddington before I set up; but I do not know.
Q. Did you not state in your own yard, to Mr. John Bardell, that you did not wish to indict the Bardells at all, but your lawyer said they must be in the indictment as he had a reason for it? A. I said I had no animosity against them—I did not say I did not wish to indict them at all—I told them Mr. Norton said they must be in the indictment, he had a cause for putting them in—I did not say I did not know the reason, but if he would meet me at Mr. Norton's I would go and see if I could not get them out—I did not go to Mr. Norton's for any such purpose—I went to Mr. Norton's on a Sunday, with Mr. John Bardell—I do not recollect his asking Mr. Norton why he had put his brothers in the indictment—I will swear it was not said in my presence—I do not recollect Mr. Norton being questioned as to whether they could not get out of it—there was very little said—we were not there ten minutes—I will swear I did not hear Mr. Norton say their names must remain in the indictment his object being to preven their being witnesses for the defence—I will swear I never heard it; nor that their evidence against them would be of importance.
Q. Have you ever said to any body that you would have times or 100 l.?
A. Never in my life—I did not know the different times when I started—I had John Lee in my service, and William Beales.
Q. Did you not direct both Lee and Beales to run in oppsition to Bind and Johnson, and nut to leave them till they had your orders, if they did not earn one shilling? A. Never—I belive they have been in every one's time, but I never gave them order's except not to go in the women's time, (there are some female properties)—that was about a month or five weeks before the prosecution commenced.
Q. Have you not sworn to Mr. Phillips that you never gave them any specific directions at all, but that they ran when they pleased, and did as they liked? A. Yes, they did—I have never, on any occasion, directly or indirectly, by myself or Mr. Nortson, sought or agreed to settle this matter if I could get money paid me—I did not, after the 8th of June, give directions to Lee to run my omnibus against Bird and Johnson.
Cross-examined by MR. KNOWLES. Q. Do you know Thomas Parker? A. Yes; he is foreman to Mr. Aldridge—I might see him about the 5th of this month, with the two Bardells, and there might be a conversation about this indictment—I told them I had no animosity against them, but their names must be in the indictment.
re-examined. Q. You were a bankrupt the first time, about seven years ago? A. Yes; I obtained my certificate about two months afterwards—I did not leave my premises—they took bills at certain dates, which were afterwards paid—the second time I was a bankrupt, was about ten months ago—I had lost 900 l. in three weeks, by bad debts, which was the case of my being a bankrupt—I got my certificate on the 15th of January—every one of my creditors signed it, who had proved—I do not know where Lee or Mann live—I live nearly oppsite Day and Martin's, within a few doors—I hardly know what the meaning of a time is—I understand it is to start three minitues before another—If my omnibus started from the Bank at ten o'clock in the morning, it would go to Paddington, and it would stop there for half perhaps—they went just as they could—that would necessarily interfere with different times.
COURT. Q. Tell us every thing that was said by you to John Bardell? A. He asked me if I would go with him to get Mr. Norton to take the bail for his brother, without some certificate, or something; I forget what; and would I go and advise Mr. Norton to do so, without giving any trouble; and I did so—he did not ask me why the Bardells were made defendants—when I went with him he complained of Mr. Norton having refused the bail, and asked whether, if he were there on Monday, prepared with the bail, he would take it; and 1 told Mr. Norton not give them any more trouble, but to take their bail—I did not want to be litigious ducting himself wrong to my buses.
ROBERT BRAY . I am the prosecutor's son. On the 8th of June, my father commenced running two omnibuses, Nos. 348 and 349—I have been coachman to both of them—I began about three weeks after they started, but I was repeatedly with them before—I rode on horseback with them—the drivers of the other omnibuses tried to oppose us and to injure us, by pulling up against us—they would cross us and go before our buses, and their conductors would jump down, if passengers bailed us, to get them away from our buses —the names of the present defendants were on those omnibuses—Bolton had five on the stones, Bardells, 1 think, had four, Richard Bird had two, Thomas Bird had two, Carpenter had two, Cowderoy had sometimes five sometimes six, Hill had two, I believe—Johnson,
I think, had three, Newell had three, Pearce had three, Parkes had three or four, Sellick had three, and Trevett two—they were all running on the road before the 15th of June, and it was them which so annoyed us—on the 15th of June, the "Albion" and the "Emperor," opposition omnibuses, were put on—I cannot exactly remember who was the coachman of the "Albion," but John Gulliver drove it, and when he did not, some odd man did; George Dodd was the conductor; John Gough has driven it, and Trevent been the conductor—I do not know his christian name—I believe it is Robert—the name on the "Albion" was Thomas Corbett, No. 177, and on the "Emperor" was the name of Edmund Sylvester—It was No. 352—the driver of that was John Sylvester, and the conductor John Smith—these two followed us every where we went, and the other omnibuses still pursued the same line of conduct as they had before—when we stopped, the opposition omnibuses stopped also—I saw that done on the 15th—about three weeks after that I began driving—I think No. 348 was the first I drove—I cannot tell which of the opposition omnibuses accompained that—I think it was the "Emperor"—but one of them did, and sometimes both of them—we started from the Wheatsheaf, at Paiddington, and went to the Bank, and the opposition omnibuses accompained us, if we stopped they stopped also—If we turned round, they would turn and follow us up the by-streets and any where else—If I met any of the other omnibuses they would stop with me also, so that I had always two with me.
COURT. Q. Tell us any street into which you turned and they followed you? A. we went at nine or ten o'clock, one Sunday evening, to Mr. Savory's, on the other side Camden-town, near the Regent's-park, on purpose to see if they would follow us—we stopped there till very nearly two o'clock in the morning—the "Emperor" followed us, and remained there all the time—John Sylvester drove it, and Smith was the conductor.
MR. BALL. Q. Did you go to Whitechapel? A. No, I went to Marylechone police-office, which is out of my beat, and the "Emperor" accompained me there, and stopped all the time I stopped—I broke down twice, and they stopped at the same time—I once broke dewn in the New-road, and once in the Edgware-road, both out of our road, and they stopped with us, and followed us home—we lengthened one of our omnibuses, No. 349, to Maida-hill—I think on the 6th of July—I drove that at time—the "Emperor" followed me that very day, and pursued the same kind of conduct us before, in running before me—In going to Maida-hill I went through Paddington—If in driving I came within Bolton's times, I had his five omnibuses with me also—he had three minute's time—I think his first time was a quarter before ten o'clock, and he had two three minutes after that—If I came within his first time he would hang back, and take a minute or two of the other man's time, and the other one would hurry to make his time up; so that I had two with me, and very likely three or four—If I came within Bardell's time, they would hang back upon me in the same manner—Richard Bird did the same—Thomas Bird the same—Carpenter, Cowderoy, Hill, Johnson, and Newell did the same—It was he followed me out of my road a day or two after the sale, up Hinde-street, and two or three other streets which I turned up to get out of their way, as they were hunting us so much—Pearce did the same—Parker and Sellick the same—l never saw Robert Trevett drive, but I have seen him on the box of the "Emperor"—I have heard him halloo to them to "Keep at him," and cheering them on—I believe the "Albion" used to put up at night at Trevett's stable, but I did not see it—they used to follow me to
the yard at night, and turn round to see if I was coming out again—they would wait for an hour, and very often for two or three hours—when I started in the morning, I have met them at the corner of Southamptom-street, waiting for me, which is within fifty yards of Red-lion-yard, but they generally used to stop at the corner of Edgeware-road—I have gone up Southampton-street, and gone the back way to Paddington, and they have followed me that way—whenever they met me they were sure to follow me—on the 4th of July I went to the White Horse after my father—I saw Cowderoy, Trevett, and Hill there—I believe they had sent for my father over there—I heard Cowderoy say, "That is right, old one, start half-a-dozen, it will soon bring it to a close; we are such b----y rum ones, and such out-and-outers" —and Mr. Hill said, "The old one is a right one, he ought to be one, he is an old hackney-coach master"—Robert Travett was present at the time, and he had some conversation with him, but I did not exactly hear what it was—they were each of them drinking a great deal, and got pushing each other about in their chairs—Mr. Trevett said, "Why don't you buy a time?"—my father said, "No, I never intend buying a time"—Mr. Trevett said, "It is no good your trying to beat us, for we are such out-and-outers you can't beat us"—I did not hear any thing said about 10 s.—Richard Bird has abused me very much on the bus —he said he wished my b—y bus was smashed all to pieces—he once said so at the corner of the New-road and once in the City—he said that after the "Zebra," Mr. Johnson's bus, had run against the pannel of our bus —I was not with it at the time, but I saw the damage that was done—I dined at Woodgate's, the Royal Oak, pretty nigh every day—I rather think I missed one day—I do not know what street it is in, but it is out of the road—we took our omnibus there, and the other accompanied us—the driver and conductor of that omnibus very often dined at the same place that we did—we did—we put up our omnibus at Mr. Davis's—we remained there sometimes half an hour, and sometimes two hours, or more, and the "Emperor" always remained exactly the same time, and started at the same time—It did not go to any yard—It remained outside the door—when we have been going along the road and had not any passengers, we have very often turned off, and supposing they had any passengers they would give them away to another bus and turn round and catch us—I have very often gone a mile or two out of my way to see if they would catch me, and they have always come after me and found me—when I have wanted to take up passengers, they have tried to get them into their own bus —I remeber our calling for Mr. Hillhouse, the surgeon, in Oxford-street, and he requested to be set down at his own door, which is in Holborn—he said that aloud, as he was sitting on the box with me, and the "Emperor" pulled up at his door, and I could not put him down there—If any ladies wanted to come in, they would try to pull before me, so that I should not pull up to the curb-stone to take them up—If a lady had a child in her arms, they would run and take the child out of her arms, and put it into their bus, that I should not get the fare—I have seen Joseph Bardell beckon for his buses to come up and follow me when I have been driving—I have heard John Sylvester say, when he has met Mr. Bolton, "There goes our governor"—Sylvester has said to me, "You had better have bought a time: you have lost a great deal of money now, and a little more would have bought a time"—he has said, "We have times to sell; we want 160 l. a piece for them"—he said Mr. Newman had lost 600 l. and he wished he had bought a time—I said we should never buy a time—he said it was no use our going on;
they should be sure to ruin us—he said they were ordered to follow us wherever we went, and to stop out till ten o'clock at night—(they very often wished me to go to Edgeware, and dared me to go forty or fifty miles into the country, and they would follow)—he has also said it was not use to run in opposition against the Paddington committee, they were so strong—I have not heard Smith say as much as that—he has said, when he has met Mr. Bolton, "That is our governor," or, "Do you see our governor?"—our conductor was Joseph Raines—we stopped on the 26th of July—they had continued to follow us till that day—I think that day they stopped at Paddington—my father came out again on the 29th, and I went out on the 30th with No. 348—the "Emperor" accomopained me then—my omnibus broke down; and when I drove the other bus the four o'clock journey, they both accompained me—If a passenger tried to get in, they would jump down, and try to persuade him to get into their bus
Q. During the time that this opposition was going on, did you see Cowderoy drive? A. Yes, several times—he drove his own bus, and his conduct was as much against me as could be to prevent my getting passengers—I have seen Newell conducting his own bus, and his conduct to me has been the same as the others, trying to get the passengers into his own bus —when I have come in their time, or before their time, they would hurry on to catch me, and then keep with me—they would run over any other man's time to keep up with me—It was Newell's that followed me out of the road.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Did Richard Bird, Thomas Bird, Cowderoy, or Johnson, ever drive out of their road against you? A. No—they are proprietors, having their own times—when I drove in their times, they ran with me; and they have come out with me, and stopped beyond their own time—I never saw either of them conduct or drive the "Albion" or the "Emperor," but they have cheered them on to go on against me—It was the "Albion" and the "Emperor" which followed me wherever I went—I have played at skittles once or twice with the men—I have played at skittles at the Royal Oak, with John Sylvester and Brush, that is Smith—I have played five or ten minutes—I have been there while the game was going on—I will swear I have not played half-a-dozen games—I have dined with Sylvester and Smith, and drank together at dinner—I went to Wales' tea-gardens, at Bayswater—they followed me there, and I think I had one game at skittles—there were seven or eight besides them; that was after business—I went there for pleasure, and to see if they would follow me—we played together, and I think drank together—I do not recollect being at any other places with them—I have drank wine with Mr. Trevett, close to his stable, at Mr. Gamble's—It was some time in June—I went down there, and Mr. Trevett asked me to take a glass of wine with him—he asked me to treat his men—he treated them with some, and I treated them with some.
Q. That was while all this wickedness was going on? A. It was at the beginning of it—I did not hear Trevett on that occasion complain that my father had set up an omnibus in opposition, when he knew that the times were filled up—I did not hear any one say so—my father has no partner—he never had one—he never drove an omnibus, but he has one now, which he bought about a fortnight after he began, at the time he was so much annoyed by the other two—I do not know exactly who he bought it of—I believe he had seen Mr. Norton several times before he bought it—I cannot say who he bought it of—he got the money by earning it—I
did not hear my father say who he bought it of—I never saw it before me father had it—I do not know that Mr. Norton had any thing to do with in—I do not know that Mr. Norton ever had any thing to do with omnibuses—I knew a man named Lyons, when we sold him horses—I do not know that he is a friend of Norton's—one of the Lyons did drive for us—I do not know whether my father bought the omnibus of him, or whether Norton found the money—I cannot tell how many omnibuses were on the road between Paddington and the Bank—when my father set up, there were between twenty and thirty; there might be more—there were tunekeepers on the road—I do not know what their business was—when my father's omnibus started, we did not go at any particular time—we went when we could get a load—we tried to start at nine o'clock, but found we could not get a load—I knew there were times at which the different omnibuses ran, but I did not know at what time they went.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. I understand your father's omnibuses started on the 8th of June, and there was no opposition till the 15th? A. Yes there was; but the "Emperor" and the "Albion" did not start till then—Newell's omnibus followed us out of the line of road—I do not know that my father's omnibus, on the first day it started, went out of the line to Market-street, near Trevett's stable, and waited till his omnibus came out, and stuck to it all day—I did not accompany my father's omnibus all the way that day—I do not know that, one thursday, one of my father's omnibuses went out of the way to Bolton's stable, and waited for his omnibus, and hunted that all day—I never heard it from my father—such a thing could not have happened on the regular road without my seeing it—I could not help driving close to some of the other omnibuses—I did not drive my pole through Mr. Gaywood's omnibus—they threw their door open, and caught my wheel, and broke their door—I did not know that there were quite enough omnibuses on that road, before my father startede his—we have not put passengers out of our omnibus, to turn and hunt these other omnibuses.
Cross-examined by MR. BODKIN. Q. You say that Newell's omnibus followed you, was he driving on that occasion? A. No.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. Have you been in your father's employ as a horse-dealer? A. Yes; I have been with him ever since I was born—I do not know that before we started, there was an omnibus went every three minutes—we live opposite Day and Martin's—some omnibuses stop there, but I do not know that I they all do—I cannot say whether there is one stops every three minutes—I went backwards and forwards with my father's omnibus—the first week I watched them, my father merely gave me instructions to keep them steady—I do not know the omnibus, No. 245—I do not know George Hoby and Robert Weston, the driver and conductor of it by name—I may know them by sight—I drove my father's omnibus latterly—I might have driven half a journey about a fortnight after they started—I never saw my father's omnibus follow No. 245, or any other omnibus, but I have seen others follow my father's—when our omnibus drove against Gaywood's, they were not running against us, as they were standing at the bottom of Holborn-hill, and they threw the door open before I had time to pull up.
EDWARD MANSELL . I went into the service of Mr. Bray on the 8th of July—I was then conductor to his L'addubgton omnibus, No. 348—I have driven both Nos. 348 and 349—I am still in his service—I drive No. 349 now—the "Albion" used to follow and accompany No. 348 wherever it
went—I know Bolton, the two Bardells, richard Bird, thomas bird, Caroebter, cowderoy, Hill, Johnson, Newell, Pearce, Parkes, Sellick, and Trevett's omnibuses—they all have obstructed and opposed me—they have drawn before me, and put their poles close to my door—sometimes I have been in one time, and sometimes in another, and whichever time I have been in they hyave gone before me—when a passenger was coming they would put their pole up to my door, so that a passenger could not get in—John Hitchings, (that is Gough,) and Thomas Trevett have abusede my inside passingers—at the corner of Regent-street—they have driven their pole close enough to alarm any female who was getting in—they have stopped before me, so as to obstruct my going on—If there were two carts, they would stop between them to prevent my passing—that has frequently been the case—I remember Mr. Bray's omnibuses being given up for a time—It was on the 27th of July—I saw Gough get off his box, and I saw Trevett go into a shop—I then saw blue ribbons on the "emperor" and "Albion"—both Gough and Trevett were with the "Albion"—I cannot say who was with the "Emperor," but I saw them driving one after the other—I have seen the "Emperoro" put up at night at Mr. Bolton's yard, in North-wharf road—I do not know whether he lived there—one night, after my bus had gone home, I rode home with Trevett and Gough in the "Albion," to Trevett's yard, and we had a glass of something to drink—that might be ten days or a fortnight before the bus was stopped—when I have been driving, Trevett has interfered with my passengers, and told them I was going to Charing-cross, by which means I have had several passengers refuse going with me—I saw persons in the omnibus when they had the blue ribbon, but I cannot say who they were—I still continue with Mr. Bray's bus, and the "Emperor" still continues to follow me.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Were you on the road before Mr. Bray started? A. I was on the Elephant and Castle road—I know ther were no opposition buses on the Paddington road.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. You drank together? A. They came to the same house, but at last we quarrelled; we then went to another house, and they followed us there—we dined in the parlour by ourselves, and then they went back to the house where they had been before; we went to Mr. Friend's, a gin-shop—when I first went I have played a game at skittles with them, and we dined together.
JAMES RAYNER . I was conductor to one of Mr. Bray's blue buses —I began on the 8th of June—we were obstructed by the other omnibuses on the road—the "Emperor" and "Albion" were put on about a week after we began—I became conductor to No. 349, which went on to Maida-hill, and the other omnibuses continued the same mode of obstruction—the "Emperor" followed me when I went out of the way—on one ocoasion it followed me up to Mr. Savory's, at Portman-town—It waited till near two in the morning—the "Emperor" followed me out of the road on several occasions; if we turned round, they turned round with us—about six weeks after I started, the "Emperor" broke the door-pannel of our omnibus with his pole—he came after us rather too sharp, and the pole ran up against our door, and broke the pannel—I saw the blue ribbons on their horses' heads in the Edgeware-road—I rode on the steps of the "Emperor" to Thomas Bird's yard, on the 27th, between eleven and twelve o'clock in the morning—I saw Mr. Hill in the omnibus—I did not notice any body else—he went and asked if Mr. Bird was at home—I do not know what answer he received—I left them at the yard.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Do not you recollect receiving orders from Mr. Bray to go against Trevett? A. I do not recollect it—he said he did not understand the business; he left it entirely to his men to go in what times they liked—I received a direction to go in whose time I thought proper—I do not recollect receiving positive directions from Mr. Bray to go against Trevett—I was conductor to the omnibus driven by Lee—I do not recollect going with Mann—there has been two Mann's at work—we did not receive directions from Mr. Bray to go against Trevett in particular—he said Trevett's time was a very good time, and so was Mr. Bird's, and wer could not do better than go in them—Lee was present when Mr. Bray gave those directions—I did not tell Teevett I had orders to go in his time, nor I did not hear Lee tell him so—I first went with the omnibus on a Monday—the regular line is from the Wheatsheaf to Bank.
Q. On that day did you go down Market-street, to the place where Mr. Trevett's omnibuses put up? A. I do not know whether it was the first day—I certainly went to a public-house there, to dine, close by where Trevett puts up—that is out of the line of road—I remember going there several times.
Q. Do you remember when you went triumphantly down Market-street, saying, "We will soon dish up the old Snob, we will soon melt his wax for him?" A. I did not say much a thing, nor hear such a thing—there was no one in my omnibus—I have not boasted that I would melt the cobler's wax.
JURY. Q. Had you orders from Mr. Bray to go down Market-street? A. No, Sir; we went where we pleased to drive, and they followed us.
MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. But you followed them when you went to their house? A. It was Mr. Gamble's house we went to—I know Trevett's men go to that house occasionally—I dined with two or three of them there that day—It is a house where coachmen and omnibus-men generally go.
Q. Do you mean to swear that they go out of their line of road? A. We were not plying for passengers—we went to have our dinner.
JURY. Q. Was the observations about the time when you first began, or afterwards? A. Afterwards.
MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Had you know Mr. Bray before you were engaged by him? A. I had never seen him—I was recommended to him by Mr. Davidson, and am in his employment still—I have been in the ring—there is not a warrant against me now for manslaughter, for killing a man in a boxing match—I do not deny that I was severn years ago at Hammersmith—I remember Paddy Flynn and another being transported—I have fought two or three battles.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. You have said the pole of the "Emperor" was driven into your pannel A. Yes; that was in consequence of my pulling up suddenly, to prevent running over somebody, I believe.
Q. Now with respect to this public-house near Trevett's stable, did you not wait there, colse to Trevett's yard, till his omnibus came out, and then go with them? A. We went with them to town and back.
COURT. Q. The question was, did you wait? A. We did not particularly wait for them—we went with them when they went.
MR. PAYNE. Q. Did you not follow one of Trevett's? A. I do not recollect seeing one of them—we came away at the same time.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. I think Lee, Mann, and Beals were servants of Mr. Bray as well as yourself? A. Yes.
Q. Have you not told Mr. Bird, when you were running your omnibuses against his, that you should not have done it but you had received orders? A. I certainly received orders to go in his time, as well as Mr. Trevett's, as it was a very good time.
Q. Now I give you notice that Lee and Beals are here; upon your solemn oath, have you not said that to Mr. Bird? A. No, Sir; I have not said any thing to him in particular—I have not told him I was sorry to oppose him—I never had any any conversation with either of the Mr. Birds about going in their times.
JURY. Q. Had you orders to go in Mr. Bird's time? A. I had.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Did you not assign that as a reason to Mr. Bird? A. No; Birds name was mentioined to me, and Trevett's and Johnson's was also mentioned by Mr. Bray.
Q. Then if Bray has sworn that he never directed any servant of his to run in opposition, or in the time of any body by name, has he not sworn what is wickedly false? A. I do not know what Mr. Bray has sworn—I worked for Mr. Bray before he bought the third omnibus—I do not know who he bouoght it of—I have not known Mr. Norton till within the last month—I have seen him about Mr. Bray's premises since that time—I have never been in any trouble myself—I have not for a long period get my living by fighting—I have fought pitched battles for money—I know Lyons, he used to be an omnibus proprietor.
Re-examined. Q. You never saw Mr. Norton till the last month? A. No—the directions I first received from Mr. Bray, were to go in what time I liked; as he did not know what time, he would leave it all to his men—about the first or second weeek after I went, he said that Bird's and Trevett's were very good times, we could not do better than to go in their times—their time was about nint o'clock in the morning, or a little after—It is just upon seven years since I fought my last pitched battle—I never killed a man in my life—I was present at a fight where a man was kille, and two men were transported for it.
HENRY SWAITZER . I am a clerk in the Stamp Office. On the 15th of June, Thomas Bolton, in the name of John Bolton, obtained a supplement license, in the name of Edmund Sylvester; and at the same time, Robert Trevett obtained a supplemental license, adding the name of Thomas Corbett to his own—the first was for the "Emperor," and the second for the "Albion;" and on the 6th of July, Bray obtained a supplemental license, to go to Maida Hill—and on the same day, Bolton and Sylvester obtained a supplemental license, lengthening the distance for the "Emperor" to Maida Hill.
WILLIAM NASH . On the 27th of July, I saw an omnibus and the horses had blue ribbons—I cannot tell the number of the omnibus, nor who drove it—I did not see either of the defendants there, to the best of my knowledge—I know Mr. Cowderoy—I do not recollect that I have drank with him—I saw him at the Feathers that day, I believe—I do not recollect seeing him in the box of the omnibus when the horses had blue ribbons—I cannot recollect who I saw—I am in the habit of standing at my gate, and seeing them go past.
SARAH TURNBULL . I was the proprietor of an omnibus, which ran from Paddington to the Bank, but I have not been so for nine months past, There was a committee of the Paddington omnibus proprietors, they called them
selves a commitee—I belonged to it before I ceased to be proprietor—I was a member of the committee—I certainly went at times, but very seldom—I went three or four times—I believe it generally consisted of the whole of the propietors of the omnibuses running from paddington to the Bank over the stones—they met at the New Inn—I have been there, and at the Lion in the Edgeware-road—I have at times received notices to go—I cannot recollect whether they were printed or written—the times of meeting were very distant indeed—I seldom or ever went—I was a proprietor from the commencement of the running on the stones, which was in 1832—I know Mr. Bolton—I have seen him at the committee, and James and Joseph Bardell, Richard Bird, Thomas Bird, Carpentor, Cowderoy, Hill, Johnson, Newell, pearce, parkes, Selick, and Robert Trevett—It only consisted of proprietors—the last time I was at the committee there was a demand made upon me for 60 l. or 70l—I ceased to be a proprietor nine months ago—I disposed of it altogether to my son, but I have not settled with him—we drew no agreement at all.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Does not that consist of your good-will obtained from running your omnibuses at certain hours from Paddington down to the Bamk? A. Certainly.
Re-examined. Q. There were certain times allotted to you to run; what times werethey, three minutes? A. Yes; I had six times allocated to me in the day—the other parties were not allowed to run at the same minutes—If any strangers have interfered, I was to do the best I could with them—I believe strangers have interfered, I do not recollect—there were arrangements made by the committee if a stranger interfered, but I can hardly tell what, as I so seldom attended—I do not recollect being there when any stranger had interfered—It is some time since I was there—I cannot tell whether, when I was there last, anything was stated by the committee of any arrangement having been made before, of what was to be done when any stranger interfered—there was none that I recollect.
BENJAMIN BALL I am a perfumer, and live in Oxford-street. I have observed Mr. Bray's omnibuses, and the conduct of the other omnibuses towards them—I know the "Emperor" and the "Albion"—we have been very much annoyed by these omnibuses running one against another—about six weeks back I wanted to get into Bray's omnibus, and the "Emperor" came right against the steps, so as to prevent my getting in—I have seen such conduct on several occasions—the "Emperor" and the "Albion" appeared to obstruct Mr. Bray's omnibuses by running—when any person wanted to get into Mr. Bray's, the "Emperor" would run across the steps, sometimes their pole came almost on the pavement.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Has Mr. Bray's gone at a quick pace along the street? A. Yes, Sir; I have seen it go at a quick pace—I have seen it run to get out of the way of the others—I have not seen it racing parallel with them, because they have gone before him or behind his—he has gone about six miles an hour—I will not swear he has not gone eight—I never knew of their doing anything improper—I should not be surprised if they had been summoned for furious driving; that is what occurs every day.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Do you mean that the "Emperor" and the "Albion" drew up? A. It was the "Emperor" that drew up—the "Albion" impedes by getting before the omnibus—I have seen them both with it—I have generally seen the "Emperor" behind, and the "Albion" before—I think so—I think there are several omnibuses called by those names.
COURT Q. You have observed these omnibuses, and the "Emperor" and "Albion" constantly; upon your oath, can you state that the "Emperor" and "Albion" have been impeding Bray's, or did Bray's annoy them? A. I have seen the "Emperor" oppose Bray's omnibus—that is what I swear to—I could not swear to the driver of the "Emperor"—I put down the number—It is 352.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. When did you make any communication to Mr. Norton? A. I do not know him—I was supcenaed to attend to give evidence—I never gave evidence but in this Court—I have talked of it to my neighbours—I saw Bray last night—I never saw him before—I did not speak to him.
MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. What is the number of the "Albion?" A. 177—I marked it on the back of my subpœna.
RICHARD TEMPLE . I am a brazier and oilman, and live in Prince's-street, Cavendish-square. I know Mr. Bray's omnibus—I have seen it when one of Bolton's omnibuses has been before it, and one of Bardell's along with it—I spoke to young Bray—he nodded to me—he then turned round and went back again, up Oxford-street, and Bolton's followed him—I told the coachman, who happened to be young Mr. Bray, that what I had seen I should be most happy to say in any Court afterwards—I have seen Bolton's following Mr. Bray's about—I saw it last Saturday.
JOHN RAWLINGS . I am a linen-draper, and live at No. 133, Oxford-street. I have noticed Bray's omnibus, in their way from Paddington, along Oxford-street, for some months—I have seen it almost daily—I have noticed the conduct of other omnibuses towards it, the "Emperor" for one—they were chasing Mr. Bray's; one was before and one behind—I cannot recollect what was the name of the other omnibus—the one before obstructed him in his buisness, and the one behind interfered with the passengers who wanted to get in—I have seen that frequently—they pulled the people away frequently—their pole was nearly close to the door, so as to prevent any person getting in—I cannot say that I have noticed any other omnibus interfere in this way but the "Emperor" and one more—Bray's omnibus was obstructed in its free course by them—I do not recollect that I have seen Bray's act improperly to them, but I have seen the opposition strong against Bray.
COURT Q. Did Bray's ever act so as to oppose them? A. I never noticed that.
WILLIAM THOMPSON DICKENSON . I am a chemist and druggist, and live at No. 147, oxford-street. I know Mr. Bray's omnibuses—I have observed them for about two or three months—I have observed two other omnibuses, with the name of "J. Bolton" on them—Chapel-place is a place where they generally stop, and if Bray's omnibus has stopped there to take passengers, or any passenger attempted to get in, the others drove in such a manner as to prevent their getting in—I have observed that frequently with those omnibuses—I cannot speak as to others—I have seen Mr. Bolton's more particularly.
COURT Q. Have you ever seen Bray's omnibuses interfering with those other omnibuses, and preventing people getting into them? A. No.
GEORGE HILHOUSE . I am a surgeon, and live at No. 14, High Holborn. I remember going by Mr. Bray'somnibus on the 18th of August—It took me up, and putu me down in Oxford-street—and on my return by it the "Emperor, Edmund Sylvester, 352, "would not allow me to get down at my own door—I suspect it was because they saw Mr. Bray take me from
my own door—they stopped to see me taken up, and I was stopped by the "Emperor" pulling up against my door, which would not allow me to get down at my own door, and I was obliged to get down in Kingsgate-street—I have seen opposition to Mr. Bray's omnibus at other times, one before and one behind it, so that I have been obliged to take another omnibus.
COURT. Q. Have you ever seen Bray's omnibus acting in this way to the others, and annoying them? A. Not in the least.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Have you attended Mr. Robert Bray? A. I have.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Hoe far had you to go from Kingsgate-street to your door? A. About ten yards.
JAMES JOSHUA AUSTIN . I am a silversmith and jeweller. I have noticed Bray's ominbus going from Paddington to the Bank repeatedly—I have noticed other omnibuses near Mr. Bray's; in my opinion, they were racing against him—I have seen one behind him, and when Bray's omnibus stopped, the other endeavoured to drive against him, in order to get his passengers—I should suppose if any timind female wanted to get into Bray's omnibus, she might have been alarmed by it; but I never saw that—I have seen one omnibus close before him as well as behind—when Bray's stopped, the others would stop—I have seen that—their conduct was such as to obstruct him in his regular course from Paddington to the Bank—I never saw any improper conduct of Bray's to the others.
JOHN MORRIS . I live at No. 7, Chapel-place, Oxford-street, and am an accountant. I have seen Bray's omnibuses, and other omnibuses following them, and with them frequently—I cannot tell the names, but I think I have seen the "Emperor"—In general one went before Bray's, and the other behind—I have been them draw up when people were getting in very frequently—I have seen more than two omnibuses with Bray's occasionally—they were acting in such a way as to obstruct Bray's—I thought it was a little rivalry.
JOHN BATTERBEE . I live at No. 133, Oxford-street, and am a shoemaker. I have observed the omnibus with the name "Aaron Bray" on it, gernerally accompanied by there others, one before, one behind, and one by the side of it—that was a very common occurrence, almost every day—generally when he stopped they put their poll against him, so as to obstruct people getting in.
COURT. Q. Did you see the parties conducting Bray's omnibus acting so? A. No.
MR. RATCLIFF. I am a carver, gilder, and picture dealer, and live in Holborn. I know Mr. Bray's omnibus, and have occasionally ridden in it—I have noticed the conduct of Mr. Bray's omnibus, and the conduct of other omnibuses with respect to him, so much so that I have been compelled to apply to the police—about two months ago I was at Maida Hill, and wished to get into Bray's omnibus, another drew up, and had I not have been as quick as possible, I think the pole would have come against my head—I did not take notice of that omnibus—It appeard to me to be done to prevent my getting into Bray's omnibus—when I have passed through Holborn, I have seen one or more omnibus accompanying Bray's; but I did not notice them, as I never expected it to go beyond self-protection about which I went to Bow-street—on the occasion I am now speaking of, the other omnibuses were coming from Maida to the City—I remember one with the name "Emperor" on it, but I have since seen there are one
or two other of the name—one of the others was No. 244, the other was 266—I believe one was Mr. Hill's, and the other was Mr. Bardell's.
Cross-examined by MR. JONES. Q. Where did you get the number? A. I took it in the first instance, and I went last night to Bow-street, to get the two numbers, as they were summoned there by me on the 11th of August—one was Hill's and the other Bardell's—I wish to say, that the driver of Mr. Hill's was most insolent—his language was most dreadful—Sir Frederick Roe said he would fine him, 5 l. the next time—the driver of Mr. Bardell's had more sense, and behaved with more propriety—I never saw any thing improper in the conduct of Mr. Bray's omnibus—I live at No. 213, Holborn, nearly faeing Day and Martin's, twenty-five or thirty-five doors from Mr. Bray's—he lives up a yard—I have known him some years, and when I rido in an omnibus, it is generally by him.
Cross-examined by MR. KNOWLES. Q. In connexion with what was it you mentioned the name of Bardell? A. The two omnibuses had blocked up the way of Bray's, to prevent my getting in, and the police summoned them—I went to prove the case—I cannot tell which of the Bardells it was—there was the name of Bardell on one of the omnibuses—they stood and blocked up the whole road—I dare say there were thirty or forty carriages which could not go on—I begged of the first man to go on, and he refused—Bardell's, I think, was the last.
Q. Then in point of fact, he could not have got forward? A. He could have given way to give space, but not quite suffcient for the others to go past—he could no get out, but still he might have accommodsted a little if he liked—he could not get back, there were twenty or thirty carriages behind him.
JOSEPH HUDSON . I am a tobacconist, and have lived in Oxford-street upwards of thirty years. I know Mr. Bray's omnibus—I have observed it interrupted by other omnibuses for the past two or three months—I have seen the "Emperor," No. 352, and the other, No. 177, with "Thomas Corbett" on it, one before and one behind Bray's, and they driven up against his steps to prevent the passengers going by him, and half a dozen others have done the same—there must have been a great deal of danger in people getting in or out of his omnibus—I have seen Bray's turn, and the others have turned about also—I have complained to the ploice repeatedly—I have complained to Martin, the officer—the other omnibuses have driven so fast that unless I had stopped them, I could not complain to their conductors about it—I have on one or two occasions stopped them, and said if they drove so fast I should take their numbers, and go to to the policeoffice.
COURT. Q. Upon those former occasions, have you seen Bray turning round, following the others, and using the same course that they did to him? A. No.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Did you call on Mr. Bolton, and propose that they should pay 10 l. a-piece, and settle the matter? A. I never called on him—I wrote him a letter, he them called on me—I did not propose that they should pay 10 l. a-piece, and settle the matter—the letter I wrote was complaining of the insolence and abusive conduct of his drivers.
Q. But I am talking about what you said to him; upon your oath, did you not propose to him that a sum of money should be paid, and the business should be settled? A. No; I did not proposem an arrangement—he proposed an arrangement; he said, he should not like to come to the bar of the Old Bailey—I said, "I think you are right, Sir, not to have it said afterwards
that you had been at the bar of the Old Bailey"—he asked what I thought Mr. Bray expected—I said he was a perfect stranger to me—I had come forward to support Mr. Bray, together with the whole of my neighbours, to put down this racing against people as they had done; and that Mr. Bray had told me he had lost, by being opposed by Mr. Bolton and others, between 300 l. and 400 l.—I stated to him first of all, that it was a most abominable thing, that the self-elected committee of Paddington proprietors should say the whole of the King's highway belonged to them, and that no person should come there unless they paidd 200 l.—he said, "I should not have run an omnibus against him had not his man come to my yard, and insulted my people, and I put on the omnibus to run hid off the road—now do you want any thing more?"
Q. How many times has your conduct been questioned in courts of justice, for gross usury, extortion, and fraud? A. Now I mean to say, upon my oath, that sucj things may have been said and counsel will take liberty to say such things, and most untruly sometimes, but nothing of that kind ever occurred—I never was before a Magistrate on any charge, not even a watch-house charge—I have never been questioned in any case in courts of justice.
Q. Do you discount bills? A. No I—do not make loans to people, and then supply them with snuff-boxes—only in a way of business, like other tradesmen—I never was a money-lender in my life—my name is Hudson, of Oxford-street, a tobacconist—I take the liberty of saying that I am notorious for not being a money-lender.
JOHN COLE . I am a chemist and druggist, and live at No. 101, High Holborn. I know Bray's omnibuses—I have observed other omnibuses near them when they beem going along the road—I suppose a thousand times—the others were driving continually before and behind them, and trying to prevent passengers, alarming ladies, and driving so close behind it as to make it dangerous for females to get in—I have never seen Bray's do so to the other omnibuses.
RICHARD PRIESTLY . I live in High Holborn, and am a bookseller. I have seen Mr. Bray's omnibuses, and other omnibuses with them, one behind and one before, quite close—I have seen that frequently—I do not know the names of the other omnibus—one was the "Emperor. "
COURT. Q. Did it appear, from what you saw of the two omnibuses, one before and one behind, that there was fair competition or unfair? A. Unfair; I think that means were made use of to prevene Mr. Bray carrying on his business fairly.
ROBERT BLOOR . I am proprietor of an omnibus. I remember on the 27th of July, seeing the "Emperor" with blue ribons to the horses, by the toll-gate—there were several persons with it, one or two, I can recollect—I saw Mr. Bolton at the gate taking money, and the eldest Mr. Trevett was at the garden gate about five minutes before—I saw Hill—I do bot recollect seeing Cowderoy—there was an observation made by Gough, who was driving the "Albion," the oppostion bus that went against Mr. Brau's—he was driving young Trevett—he amde an observation loud enough for people to h ear, I should think—he said, "We shall be with you tomorrow"—I made an appeal to the gentlemen with em and said, "So long as you continue with me I don's care for the committee"—I have hasd oppostion against me, but not by Trevett.
Q. Who was it? Cowderoy's omnibus was driven against me one day,
about a month after—there was no omnibus put against me on the morrow, as Gough said.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. You say Boton was at the gate, taking money? A. Yes; I am told he is collector of the tolls there.
JOHN COLE . I am landlord of Mr. Corbett, in Lamb's-buildings, Barbican—he pays 20 l. a year—he is not in arrears—I believe he is in the shoe line.
A verdict of acquittal was here taken for James Bardell and Joseph Bardell.
MESSRS. PRENDERGAST, PHILLIPS, CLARKSON, KNOWLES, and BODKIN addressed the Court and Jury in behalf of the defendants, and called the following witnesses.
JOHN TREVETT . I am son of the defendant, Robert Trevett. He has some omnibuses which run from Paddington to the Bank—I remember Mr. Bray starting two omnibuses—I do not remember the day of the month, but I saw them at the Wheatsheaf on Whit-Monday—John Lee was the driver of one of them, and I believe h is conductor was Jem Raines—the driver of the other was Henry Mann, better known by the name of Cuddy Mann, and James Trevett, who is a cousin of mine, was the conductor—he had once been in my father's employment some time ago—Lee had been in my father's service, but had left, and Mann had been employed to do odde work when other men were away, or ill—the first I saw of Brau's buses was at the Wheatsheaf, and when one of my father's buses started from the Wheatsheaf, one of Bray's started with it—I think Lee drove that—my father's bus started at its regular time—my father has has omnibuses on that road about five years, I should think—when Shillibeer started in September, he started the first omnibus about January—on the day Bray's omnibuses appeared, my father's omnibus went down to the Bank, I suppose—Robert Willis drove it—I cannot recollect who was the conductor—I did not see Bray in the course of that day—I saw my father's omnibus, shout five o'clock that day, come down to the stable, in Market-street, Paddington, and Bray's omnibus followed my father's down to the stable, and passed by—that was out of the regular line of road—my father's omnibus stood outside the horses went into the stable, and staid there about forty minutes—It was tea-time.
Q. Did the conductor or driver of Bray's omnibus say any thing while they were following your father's to the stable? A. Yes; as they passed they said, "We will break the b----y cobler; we will melt his b----y wax"—that was either the driver or the conductor—I believe it wa Cuddy Mann—my father is a bot and shoemaker, and lives in Finasbury—I remember my father's omnibus starting again that eventing—while we had been waiting, Bray's buses were at the Fountain Abbey, the public-house at the corner—our buses always go roung that corner, and as soon as our buses turned that corner, I saw Bray's men get up in haste and follow them our—two buses went from the yard together, and both Bray's followed them—they started from the Wheatsheaf, at Paddington, which is about half a mile from the Fountain Abbey.
Q. Have you ever observed your omnibuses and Bray's on the line of road? Q. Yes; I think on Whit-Tuesday, the 9th of June, I saw Bray's bus and my father's in Regent-circus, Oxfort-street—that is the place they generally call at—the Magistrates will not let them stand there, so they trail gently bu; but they were thenstanding at the corner of Oxford-street—I saw a gentleman passenger in the act of getting into my father's bus, and Lee, who was then conductor to Bray's bus, went to my
father's bus, and pulled the passenger away, and put him into Bray's bus —he put his hands round the gentleman, and pulled him right away—I was not near enough to hear any thing said—Lee and the conductor of my father's bus got fighting through it—the fight did not last above a minute—some one parted them—the omnibises then went off together—I believe ours was a little first—they were very nearly abreast—the "Albion, "No. 177, was my father's bus —he has no partner in any way—I have seen Bray's and my father's racing together since the time I spoke of, but not to notice any particular circumstance.
Q. Do you know there is a Paddington committee? A. I do not—there is what is erronecously called a committee, a meeting of the proprietors of that road—they meet on Thursday nights at the White Lion—I have been at the meeting—It is held in the first floor front room—any one may walk in—there has been no secrecy when I was present—there is very seldom any thing done there at all—sometimes some of the preprietors would come and tell another that his men had been kincking up a disturbance on the road—It was always settled between the person who made the complaint, and the person whose servants were complained of—I have known of drivers being discharged by the proprietors on account of—their misconduct—I cannot say who it was—the subscription that was paid was half-a-crown a week for each member—It was to pay the time-keepers—there is no other subscription among the proprietors that I know of—the half-crown was not paid there in general—one of the conductors pays for his three omnibuses down Oxford-street—there are several time-keepers—one is deputed to receive the subscriptions, and pay the others—I never knew of any subscrition between my father and the other proprietors, to support any opposition while Mr. Bray was running his omnibuses—I have the sole management of my father's omnibus business, and had it at that time—If there had been any subscription made to oppose Bray, it must have come under my knowledge.
Cross-examined by MR. SERGEANT BUMPUS. Q. This which is "erroneously called a committee" meets at the White Lion? A. Yes—I do not know that I have ever omitted going for the last four months—sometimes there are only two or three of the proprietors there—It is called the Paddington Committee—I do not know of any jurisdiction that it has—the proprietors who formerly fixed the times go to that meeting—they all go occasionally there—I never knew of any secretary—my father has had an omnibus, called the "Tally Ho," on that road for some time—that was put on against a man's omnibus of the name of Lewis, and run him down last autumn—I know the name of George Craig—I never knew he was a proprietor—I know Shillibeer personally—I do not remember his being run against—not for six months, I do not—I remember an omnibius with the name of Newman on it; that was run against, and run off, and he sold it to Bolton, I believe—Bolton set up an opposition against him, I believe.
Q. Did not Bolton's always follow Newman's wherever it went? A. I have seen it before it, and behind, and on the same line of road—It was one of Bolton's buses —I do not remember the name of it, or who was the driver on conductor—Mrs. Turnbull was a proprictor up to the last nine months—I never remember seeing her name on a bus. nor her attending the committee—I did nto attend them rugularly till the last Christmas—I have bees there before—I do not remember seeing her there—I do not remember her having an omnibus run against her.
Q. I ask you whether the very same omnibus that ran against Newman was not put on against Mrs. Turnbull? A. Never, to my knowledge—I
never was present where Mrs. Turnbull was, nor when any thing was said about a demand of 50 l. upon her, or any other sum.
Q. What is the expense of running an omnibus from Paddington if you get no customers? A. About 14 l. a-week; it depends on the number of journeys it runs, and whether a man is fortunate in his cattle; that is for one bus, five journeys a-day—I do not know how many proprietors there are—I never reckoned them up—I know Thomas Evans and George Bingham.
Q. As far as I understand, your being at Regent-street was a mere accident? A. I was there for the purpose of watching—I think it was after dinner—I thought I woulde take a walk down the the road, to see in what manner Bray worked his bus against my father.
Q. You say that you believe the number of your father's omnibus was 177: just recollect, was not the No. 177 upon the "Albion" that was put against Bray on the 15th? A. I will not swear to that being the number on that day—theree is more than one bus of my father's on the road—when a new license is taken, there is not always a new number—Thomas Corbett is a boot-clicker—he is foreman to my father in his shop—I remember the time when it was thoought that Bray was off the road, when his horses went for sale—I do not know that the "Albion" had the horses arrayed in blue ribbons—I supped that night with the people at the White Lion—my father was present—I did not see Bolton there, nor Cowderoy—he might have been there without my seeing him—there were a great number of persons—the room was full—they were the coachmen and the conductors of any omnibuses, I believe—I do not know who paid—I never saw my father pay any thing—I do not believe he did pay any thing—I do not think he could pay without my knowing it—I sat close to him—I did not pay any thing,
Q. Tell me somebody that was there beside your father—was Gough there? A. I think he was—I did not see Sullivan there—I do not know Smith—I did not see either of the Bardells, nor Carpenter, nor either of the Birds—I do not know that Johnson was there, nor Hill, nor Pearce—he might be there—Thomas Evans was there, to the best of my knowledge—the room was full of a number of people.
Q. Am I to understand that you do not recollect any person but Gough, your father, and Evans? A. I will not swear to any other person being there—I was there, I should think, an hour and a half—I had a glass of gin and water after supper—there was nobody in the chair that I know of, or at the head of the room; it was all confusion, sometimes one sat at my right hand, and sometimes another—my father sat next to me on my left hand, part of the time—I cannot tell who sat at my other hand any part of the time—I do not know who paid—I understood that Evans and Cowderou's omnibuses had had a quarrel, and had been racing together for a few days, and had settled their difference, and they had a supper over it—I did not see blue ribbons on the horses in the "Albion. "
Q. Did you not individually give a sum of money towards that supper in the morning? A. No—I did not see my fathe give any thing—he goes down in the City in the morning, and from time to time he goes with the bus —I cannot tell whether Cowderoy does—I have seen him on buses, driving—my father's first time in the morning, is about twenty minutes after nine o'clock, and he has three times—his next journey is about twelve o'clock, or a few minutesw after, and his next time about a quarter before three o'clock—the "Albion," which runs against Bray, came to my father's yard every night.
Q. Does he continue from that time up to this to have the same three times? A. He has three omnibuses running down Oxford-street—one does not run with the other two the whole day, two follow cach other every time—the first of all that he has starts before twenty minutes after Bine o'clock, about a quarter of an hour or twenty minntes before it—I do not know the precise time that the buses start, I trust that to the men—the second bus; is about twenty minntes after nine o'clock, and the third twenty—three minutes after—we have the same times now for two of them, but one was shifted about a fornight ago.
Q. What I want to know is this., whether you have continued these three first omnibuses at these times, up to within a fortnight? A. Yes; we have—my father has always run an omnibus at those times—It has not always run in his name—one has been running latterly with Corbett's name on it.
Q. Whenever Bray set out, at whatever time, nice, ten, or eleven o'clock, did not that omnibus with Corbett's name on it set out, whenever Bray's. out? A. Yes.
COURT. Q. And did it not confine itself to those times? A. At first it did—our "Albion" time changed after Bray's.
MR. SERGEANT BOMPAS. Q. Your omnibus always went with one of his? A. Bray's went with one or other of our omnibuses—both Bray's went with one of ours from Whit—Monday to about Wednesday, with the exception of one journey—after that there was only one of Brav's with ours—the first day his started with the second omnibus we had that morning.
Q. When you say that Bray ran with one of your omnibuses, whenever it went, was not that the one that had Corbett's name on it? A. No; Corbett's name was put on about a week after Whit—Monday—Bray's continued to run against one of my father's which had not Corbett's name on—I have seen it at different times of the day—one day at one time—some times at another—sometimes he stopped at the same time we did, and sometime he went on, just as it suited his own fancy—he did that from the very beginning—those which I term the regular buses kept on at the regular time—the others stopped with him.
Q. Did you ever send your horses down to H olborn, to change the horses in that one that ran against Bray's? A. Yes, occasionally; only to that one, that was the "Albion, "No. 117, I believe, but we have two or three "Albions"—I do not know that that "Albion" ever stopped for Bray's omnibus, when it was in Bray's yard—I have not the slightest notion that our omnibus ever stopped in Holbom or Southampton-street, while Bray's was in the yary—It was sent to Holborn, because Bray's men said they would not give them time to change, they would make that pair of horses work all day long, when Bray's bus was working against our regular one—If they saw the bus he but on to protect our regular once, they would turn down some of the back streets, and if we stopped he would pop out of some of the streets.
COURT. Q. What did they do? A. They turned down the streets to lose the bus we put on to protect our regular ones, and would wait, and watch till they saw our regular buses coming, and then go on in front of them, to pick up the passengers which they would have, and we had nothing to protect ourselves with, if this other bus had not kept with them.
Re-examined by MR. JONES. Q. How soon after Mr. Bray put on his omnibus did your father put on an extra one? A. My father did not, I put it on—It on—Bray's ran against our buses when they came down by our stables—I saw they were waiting there—I sent this extra bus out, and
gave the coachman directions, "if Bray's omnibus runs against ours, you run against his—If he don't interfere with us, you come home again, and don't interfere with him"—that was on the Monday.
Q. Supposing Bray had not opposed any of your omnibuses in the way you have stated, should you have put on an extra omnibus? A. No; if he had not run agiust our omnibuses, we should not have put the extra one on against his.
COURT. Q. Do you mean if he had not put it on at all, or run in the manner he did? A. If he had not run in the manner he did againat us—If he bad run against other proprietors, it was other people's business.
MR. JONES. Q. Before you put on that extra omnibus, had you or your father, to your knowledge, consulted with any other proprietors about putting it on? A. No, we had not—In fact, my father never gave me orders to put it on, I put it on my own account.
COURT. Q. Do you mean this "Albion, 177?" A. Yes; I took it off the New-road to do it—my father has three omnibuses on the New-road, and I took that off, to the stones—the New-road omnibus had been paying its way, and this was a losing concern, but I took it off to proteet the others—I took that one because there was no other omnibus in the yard fit to go to work.
MR. JONES. Q. Did you put on another in the New-road? A. Yes, most certainly.
COURT. Q. When did you start this? A. On the Monday—Bray had been three joureys against our regular omnibuses that day—I then sent this extra one out—he went down with our buses the fourth joumey—both of his continued for about three days to run at our regular hours—then one of Bray's left us—the other still continued at the regular hours—his buses sometimes stopped where ours did—I gave the men orders, if he did not go with ours, not to go with him; but he continued in our time for about a fortnight—some of the men told me that he atarted from one startiong-point in the road, and would go a little way, turn a street, and come out in front of another; and they asked me what they were to do—they told me Bray's men dared them to come with them—I said, "Well, if they dare you to go with them, go with them wherever they go. "
ROBERT WILLIS . I am a driver in Me. Trevett's employ. I drove the "Old Albion," No. 174—the first time it went was at twenty-one minutes past nine o'clock; the next was three minutes past twelve o'clock; and the next, twelve minutes before three o'clock—I remember White-Monday—I went off that morning, at my usual time, from the Wheatsheaf, at Paddington—there was another omnibus of ours that went down the yard with me, or nearly so—Mr. Trvett's omnibus, the "Triumph," was a there—when I got to the Wheatsheaf, I saw, in two or three minutes, two omnibuses, with the name of "Aaron Bray" on the side—I had about nine minutes to stop—they came up, and saud, "Now for the old cobbler; we will give him a turn"—this was at the Wheatsheaf door—when I started at my time, one of Bray's omnibuses atarted with me; and where I atoppes, they stopped—I first stopped at the corner of the New-road, at the Crown—Bray was before me at that time—our omnibus always stops there—Bray stopped there till I started, and then he stopped too—I went next to the New Inn, and stopped there; and Bray stopped too—he was generally before me—when I stopped, or went on, he stopped or went on too—If passengers came to my omnibus, and I stopped, he would pull up
and wait to see whether I went on—we pulled up for a passenger if I was first, they got into mine, and if Mr. Bray's was first, they got into his—there was no opposition offered by Bray's men, not pulling people away—I stopped at Regent-street, which is the usual place for stopping, and Bray stopped there—our other omuibus, the "Triumph," followed me three minutes behind—I did not see it, but that is the time—I stopped opposite Day and Martin's—there is a batter's shop only ten or twelve doors from Bray's stables—we have stopped there ever since the road has been established—Bray's still kept with us all the way to the Rank—It came in behind me—every omnibus is allowed three minutes there—he took a turn round Cornhill, and as I came from the corner of the Bank, he came from the corner of Cornhill at the same moment, and kept on with me in the same way, all the way up to Paddington—where I stopped, he stopped—John Lee was the driver—he had been in the service of Mr. Trevett—the conductor was Jem Raines, I believe—I had not known him in any other character, but I heard he had been a fighting man—he had been a conductor before, I believe, but not employed by Mr. Trevett—I took my second journey at three minutes past twelve o'clock that day—Bray's, that went with me the first journey, went the second—he proceeded in the same way as he did on the first—we have regular customers that go by the omnibuses—we carry them one day, and perhaps not again for a week—we have not a regular body to ride down in the morning, and ride back again—I was followed all that journey just in the same way, by the same John Lee.
Q. Was there any expression besides having this turn-up with the old cobbler? A. The first was, "We will give the old snob a turn"—when we came from our third journey, we went to Mr. Trevett's stable, which is very nearly a quarter of a mile out of the road, and they followed us to the stable, huzzaing, and saying they would give the old cobbler a turn as they passed by—they said they would melt his wax before they had done with him—that was said so that any person who was standing on the ground might hear it—It was ballooed out from the top of their omnibus—they were hallooing out, "We will melt the old cobbler's wax before we have done with him"—that was in Market-street, where our stables are—we went there to take the horses home—they had no reason to go, for their horses stand in Holborn—I went home to tea in Star-street, and when I came back, Aaron Bray's omnibus was waiting at the public-house, near the corner of our yard, till we went out—when I got on the omnibus, I drove away, and they followed me directly—I went to the Wheatsheaf, to go the same journey again, and they pursued the same course again—I am positive as to their going on monday, Tuesday, and Wednesday; but they might omit one journey on Tuesday or Wednesday—they conducted their omnibus so as not to leave ours—I conducted mine at the usual pace to keep the time to a minute, as I did before—some time after this, one was taken away, but they kept on with us till the following Monday—they only went one journey on the Sunday, unless it was very late—not at my time,
Cross-examined by MR. WHITEHEAD. Q. When did one go away? A. One was taken away on Thursday, the 11th, but Jhon Lee remained—he worked in with me at my times, and also worked in at other times—sometimes he would go a little out of times, but three times a day he went with me, but it all depended upon whether he got any thing—he went three times with us, but if he did not get any thing, he might shift away
into other times—he might hang back a little—the omnibuses all stop at the Crown, and all start from the Wheatsheaf, unless they are a little too late, they all stop at the New Inn—they do not stop at the corner of Regent-street, but just traid along there—on the first morning, Bray got first to the Crown, and to the New Inn—I will not be positive which got first to Regent-street—we passed each other—If one stopped to take up a passenger, the other passed by—I never drove in oppositiom—I drove the regular omnibus—I heard that Raines had been a conductor before—my conductor was Dodd—he has been in Trevett's employ—I never heard that he had been a prize-fighter—I have been dirver to Mr. Trevett two years last May—I have kept the time I now keep for nine months—I never drove in opposition, but one day, that was the "Albion"—I do not recollect the number of it—It was against Mr. Bray.
COURT. Q. When you drove the "Albion" that day, what conduct was pursued by them or you? A. Ipursued the course that he had done to me before, retaliating—I waited at the Crown to see whether Mr. Bray's omnibus came to oppose Mr. Trevett's—It did come, and I went with it, and followed it—It he started and I started—he went first—Thomas Lee, his driver, gave me a challenge, and said, if he was game to Woolwich, was I game to follow him—I said, "I am game to follow you wherever you like to go"—he did not go to Woolwhich, but he ran up and down the streets, and I followed him about, out of the regular track—there was no obstruction against me for three days—they never ran their pole against me, so that passengers could not get in—I never saw any thing of the sort.
JURY. Q. By whose orders did you do that? A. Mr. Jhon Trevett told me I was to drive that omnibus that day, and that was all.
AARON MARTIN . In June last, I drove the "Triumph" omnibus, belonging to Mr. Robert Trevett, from the Wheatsheaf to the Bank and back—I remember Mr. Bray's omnibuses starting on Whit-Monday—on that morning I saw them standing at the corner of New Church-street—we stand at the corner of Old Church-street, the Edgeware-road separates them—I pulled up at the corner of Old Church-street, and John Lee and Henry Maun, the drivers of Mr. Bray's buses, said, "We have come to melt the old cobbler's wax" —I started twenty-four minutes after nine o'clock, and Henry Mann started with me—we were in a line, he at the corner of New Church-street, and I at the corner of Old Church-street—when I stopped at the corner of Oxford-terrace, he stopped with me—when I stopped at the corner of Uxbridge-road, near the New Inn, (Where we stop,) he stopped exactly with me—my omnibus stood close to the curb, and he stopped right against me—when I stopped at the corner of Regent-street, he stopped with me—when Itook up a passenger, he passed me—when he took up one, I passed him—we went on to Day and Martin's—I pulled up on the near side of the road to the White Horse, No. 100, and he pulled up on the opposite side—he stopped near Bray's stables in coming up, he did not in going down—I went on to the Bank, and took my three minutes—he did not take any time, but went into Cornhill, and plied for passengers, and when I came off, he started and pulled against me down the Poultry—I went back as far as Day and Martin's—he there changed horses, and I lost him, and did mot have him any more—the second journey he went down with Mr. Cowderoy's bus.
JOHN MARRS . I have been in the service of Mr. Trevett, as coachman, above two years—before Whit-Monday I drove up and down the New-road
I afterwards was appointed to drive the "Albion" by Mr. John Trevett—I always understood he had the sole management of the business—I began to drive on Whit-Monday, on account of Mr. Bray's omnibus coming, and waiting at the public-house till Mr. Trevett's came out, and then going with them—I saw that myself, as the omnibuses which go the New-road, and those which come down Oxford-street and Holborn, go the same way down part of the Edgeware-road—I saw Mr. Bray's omnibus, on Whit-Monday, come down to Mr. Trevett's stables, and wait there, which is out of the regular line of road, in Praed-street—I heard them say, they meant ruining the old cobbler if they possibly could; that was the main expression, they talked about melting his wax, and other expressions—I began to drive on Whit-Monday—I observed Mr. Trevett's regular omnibuses on that day—when they started, Mr. Bray's started, and then I went as well—on the Tuesday I saw Mr. Bray opposite Day and Martin's—I heard him say, "I mean sticking to the old cobbler, old Trevett, because his times are good ones"—I drove the whole of that week—sometimes I was first, sometimes Bray was—when I pulled up for a passenger, his driver would put his pole against my door, and not allow a passenger to get in—that was done half-a-hundred times, and more than that—they carried on the opposition to equally the same length that I did—their pole was put so as to obstruct my passengers—It was mpossible for them to get in;
