and if a passenger hailed me, they would run their pole against me—If the passengers did not get out of the way, it would knock them down—I remember, at Regent-street, there was a gentleman about to get into my omnibus, and John Lee prevented him, and he got a black eye from the conductor of my bus —Lee was preventing him from getting into my bus, by words, and by force as well—the gentleman had hailed me—I pulled up, and he was going to get into my bus, and they drove the pole right against the door—I pulled onh one side, and Lee followed the passenger, and prevented him getting into my omnibus by geting before him—he put out his arms, but did not lay hold of him.
MR. SERGEANT BUMPUS. Q. You drove along the New-road? A. Yes, till five o'clock on the Monday—I then had orders to go to the Wheatsheaf; and if Mr. Bray's omnibus went with Mrs. Trevett's, I was to go on; if not, I was to return—I ran the whole of that week, when they ran against Mr. Trevett's—I did not follow them wherever they went—I only made one journey that night—I do not know that there was a time to spare that night—I do not know that there were three minutes, and only two omnibuses to run—I was sent out with that bus to protect our other buses; to go if they went, and to come back if they did not go with our buses our usual regular omnibuses went the next morning, and I was there in waiting—If Mr. Bray's went with Mr. Trevett's, I was to go with him—I was an extra omnibus that morning—Mr. Bray's started, I started, and of course I went at the time of one of our other omnibuses—our first "Albion" started first—Mr. Bray followed him—then I followed, and then the "triumph;" and there was another of Mr. Bray's, behind the "Triumph"—Mr. Bray's was between the two "Albions" when he started—that continued a short distance down the Edgeware-road—we continued within a few yards of each other; sometimes I was first, some times he was—willis drove the other "Albion"—we staid about the same length of time at Regent-street, and then went on abreast together—I cannot recollect who went first—my "Albion" was No. 177—I did not always drive at the same time that Bray's omnibus
did, all that first week, because, in some journeys, he did not go against Mr. Trerett's, and then I did not go with him—on the second day, Bray's omnibus went three journeys out of the five, at the same time that Mr. Trerett's did; and I did not go more than three journeys with him that day—I went with one of his omnibuses every day in that week—when I was driving on the New-road, I went from the Wheatsheaf; my first time was twelve minutes and a half past nine o'clock; the second, six minutes past twelve o'clock; and the third, about eight minutes before three o'clock—I went my third journey on that Monday—on the Tuesday I saw Robert Brayat Day and Martin's, about twenty-five minutes past one o'clock—I was driving the omnibus—he ws driving Aaron Bray's against me—I do not know his number—Willis was driving thr regular omnibus, the other "Albion"—I went to the Bank on Wednesday morning—Bray's omnibus took his time there—I do not know that he was summoned for it—I saw him take his time—he turned in; and the man that calls the time called his time, and he went.
HENRY MANN . I drove the omnibus that had "Aaron Bray" on the side of it—I drove it the first time it started out of Bray's yard—I had previously been occassionally employed by Mr. Trevett—there were two omnibuses started from Mr. Bray's yard on Whit-Monday—Mr. Bray was there when they started—he did not say how we were to drive on the first morning—we went up to Paddington—on the Tuesday morning Mr. Bray gave us directions to keep in the same time as we worked in the first day—(that was Trevett's time)—he did not mention any person's name—on the Thursday morning the conductor told me, while Mr. Bray on Tuesday was there, to stick to Bolton's time—I had told Mr. Bray on Tuesday that I had stock to Trevett's time all day long, on the Monday, and he told me to stict to him again, and he would have his horses ready to change—the omnibus that I drove ran with Mr. Trevett's on the Monday, Tuesday, and Wednesday.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. You have been telling my friend a good deal of the instructions Mr. Bray gave you respecting Trevett—do you remember on the Thursday after you commenced, driving out of the regular line of road? A. Yes, we went up to Bolton's table, we fetched one of his omnibuses out of the yard, and followed it—we went on in opposition to Trevett and Bolton about eight or nine days—after that I left Bray's service without notice—I remember after that I left him he called son horseback at the mews where I lived—I told him I had a summons to attend to—I had been summoned to Marlborough-street for one of my own men who drove a cab —Bray then addressed himself to my conductor, and he offered Bray a shilling for the earnings of the omnibus, which was all he had got—Bray said, d—n the 1 s., it was not money he looked after, but the shabby way in which he left him, and if he got into any trouble he would see him out of it—I was discharged—I was foned 40 s., at High-street after I left Mr. Bray, for a cab of mine—I am quite sure it was by Mr. Bray's direction that I drove against Mr. Bolton—I did not notice who drove Mr. Bray's omnibus after I left.
Cross-examined by MR. SERGEANT BUMPUS. Q. Have you driven for any of the defendants? A. No, ne'er a one in the box—I have driven one or two journeys along the line of ropad for Goldburn, but I have never driven for Bolton since I left Mr. Bray, to the best of my recollection—I have been but about four journeys since I left Mr. Bray—I might probably
have gone one journey for Mr. Bolton—I believe that was the next Sunday evening after I left Mr. Bray—I was here yesterday, waiting outside—I know the policeman Bates, No. 7—I saw him here yesterday—I am not held to bail in reapect of a man of the name of Goose—I am not under any bail—I have been before a Magistrate lately, on account of Mr. Goose; he swore his life against me, but the Magistrate would not believe him—I was not present when any body struck Bates yesterday—I never saw any body strike him—I was not present when bates struck any body—he pushed him out of the way—It was not because something was said about being a witness for the prosecution.
JOSEPH GAMBLE . My father keeps the Fountain Abbey, Market-street, and I manage the business—It is the house to which Trevett's omnibus drivers usually come. On Whit-Monday I was there, and two persons who did not usually come, came there with Mr. Bray's omnibus—I cannot say what they said they would do, but they had refreshment at our house—they asked for ten, and that not being ready, they called for apint of ale, and a screw of tobacco—the tea was got ready for them—I brought it; while that was done, Trevett's omnibus started—they left their tea behind, and started off.
---- DEDMAN. I live in Market-street, and am a piano-forte-maker, On Whit-Monday I went into the Fountain Abbey, a little before five o'clock—I saw Bray's omnibus stopping there—two of Bray's men called for tea—they called for ale, and while it was getting ready, I was sitting in the front parlout—the forms were out at the window—they saw Trevett's bus go, and they left their tea and went after it—I was there on the Wednesday—I saw Bray's omnibus in Market-street—It passed Mr. Trevett's stable—the men were huzzaing and said, "You are not game to follow us"—and "We will run the b—y cobbler down" or "off"—I have seen Brauy's omnibus occasionally, and observed its proceedings—when people were going into Trevett's omnibus, the conductor of Bray's omnibus has got down from Bray's and laid hold of the people that were going into Trevett's—I have seen that several times—I have seen nothing else done ij particular—as it regards one running before tha other, that has been a common thing—sometimes Bray's bus before the "Emperor," sometimes the "Emperor" before that.
Cross-examined by MR. WHITEHEAD. Q. When was it you saw the conductor of Bray's bus pull a passenger from Trevett's bus? A. I cannot say the day—It was in the Edgeware-road—It might be four or five days after the starting—It might be eight—It was between two and five o'clock in the afternoon when I saw this, as that is the only time that I have an opportunity or witnessing their conduct, my employment being at my father's in Marylebone-street, and I go to my dinner for two hours—I do not know who was the driver or the conductor of Trevett's omnibus—I think the name "Albion" was on it—I cannot say whether the "Emperor" omnibus was there—I did not see any omnibus beside "Albion", and Mr. Bray's—Mr. Trevett's was first—Bray's conductor got down from the bus, and endeavoured to prevail on the people to get into his bus —he laid hold of them.
Q. Was it a man or a woman? A. I have seen him take both.
Q. But on this particular time? A. I did not say any particular time; as I stated before, it was five, or six, or eight days after they began the running.
Q. Was it a man or a woman upon that occasion? A. I saw him take both.
Q. Are you sober now? A. Yes, Sir—I cannot say any particular day that this took place—I told you that before, but it was at the time I am in the habit of being in the Edgeware-road.
Q. What time did you see them on the Wednesday? A. About five o'clock—one of them was then driving past Mr. Trevett's—there were several persons visiting at Mr. Trevett's; persons belonging to the yard—I think it was as near five o'clock as could be—that is the time they come in to have their tea—Trevett's bus was in, and Bray's drove round, and came past.
JAMES BATES . I am a City police-sergeant No 7. On Whit-Monday I was on duty in Cheapside, the Poultry, Lombard-street, Cornhill, and round the Bank—I do not recollect any particular dat that I observed Trevett's or Bray's omnibuses, but within a few days of Whit-Monday, seeing there were two new omnibuses, I took notice of them—they tried to take their time at the Bank—I receive orders at the Bank, that if there are any new ones, not to allow them to pull in at the Bank till they had permission, I believe from the Ward authorities.
Q. What did you observe Bray's omnibuses do? A. They tried to pull in—I remonstrated with them, and told them they must not do so till they had been before the Police Committee—I remonstrated with the driver the first day, and on the second day of his starting I believe Mr. Bray came down himself—I told him my instructions were not to suffer him to pull in, till he had been before the committee—he insisted upon pulling in, and the time keeper told him also that he could not—he insisted upon doing it, and I believe the time-keeper summoned him on the following Saturday.
Q. What was the effect of Bray's omnibuses trying to pull in upon the other omnibuses? A. It caused a great deal of confusion—by direction of the Bank there are three allowed to stand on the stand at one time, and only two during the payment of dividends; consequently, if there are more than three coms on, we order the last one off—Bray's happened to be the last on several occasions, and we ordered him off.
COURT. Q. Supposing he had been the first, would he have been ordered off then? A. No, Sir; the first week he would have been ordered off, but after that he got permission.
MR. JONES. Q. On any occasion when any of Mr. Bray's omnibuses were there, did you hear any thing respecting Mr. Trevett's? A. I heard various conversations among the man—they said, "We intend to run with you", meaning the drivers of Mr. Trevett's—"We are going to melt the old cobbler's wax now"—"He shall not have it all his own way, and we will let him know it"—I have heard Bray's omnibus drivers say that to Trevett's—Colson, the time-keeper, was there at the time, and I believe Budd was there—the time-keeper summoned Bray's driver for obstruction—after the first week they were allowed to follow the omnibus out that they had followed in but sometimes they would not wait—the men would leave the omnibus and go away to get their refreshments—from my observation, they appeared to run against Trevett's omnibus, and they did that for some time after they had permission to take time—I once had occasion to lay hold of Bray's horses—I was coming from Threadneedle-street one Friday evening, and Mr. Bray was being remonstrated with—he would not come in behind—he insisted on cutting in, and I laid hold of his horses and backed them.
MR. PAYNE. Q. Did you see Bray's come with or behind any other omnibus besides Trevett's? A. Yes, with several of them that have been
down first in the morning—I have seen one of Bray's run against Bolton's—they all conducted themsoleves pretty much alike—I complained before the Police-committee that Bray's did not conduct themselves fairly.
Cross-examined by MR. BALL. Q. Do you remember being at the White Lion, on the evening of the 27th of July? A. I know the house, but I never was in it in my life—I backed Bray's horses once, his omnibes was half across the road—I think on that occasion the two ominbuses that were in, were Mr. Bird's—they stood one behind another, and Mr. Bray's wanted to come in between them—young Mr. Bray was driving—I did not see the "Albion" or the "Emperor" at that time, they were not in Bank-street, they might he in Pinees-street—this was about half-past six o'clock in the evening—It might be three weeks or a month after Bray came ont he road—when Bray's omnibuses came in, they were generally in company with the "Emperor" and the "Albion"—the "Emperor" was generally before Bray, and the "Albion" behind—the omnibuses of all the defendants came in that direction—I have seven six or seven of them come in that way—I never went to Mr. Bray's, passengers, and told them it was dangerous for them to go in—I said to young Mr. Bray, the day after the sale, "I thought you sold off yesterday, and had done with it," and he went and reported it to his father, who brought me up for defamation—the Magistrate dismissed it.
JOHN GYNN ( City police-constable No. 13.) I am stationed round the Bank. I remember when Bray's omnibuses first made their apperance—they appeared to me to be running direct against Trevett's—I saw Mr. Braw and his men frequently—I have heard the man say to Trevett's men that they would melt the old cobber's wax —they would say to them, "We will be with you, you b—s," and use very bad language; and somtimes they made use of the word snob—I do not know that they talked of any body else beside Trevett—this violent opposition went on, I should think, about a week—Bray's omnibuses did not take their time, and take their turn as the others, when they first started—they afterwards had time given them—they did not keep their time as other omnibuses did—they used to come in, and go round Cornbill, and ply for hire, if they could get passengers; if they did not, they wanted to come in and take time—that occasioned a disturbance, because they would want to cut in betwixt the three omnibuses which were allowed to stand at the Bank—I have found it necessary to interface, by their coming in irregularly, wanting to cut in, and throw the others out, which almost endangered the people's lives, by thowing omnibuses and horses on the pavement—they have come in at times and taken their times regularly; at other times they have not—they have gone out in all kind of ways; at other times taking times—they have gone to the corner of Cornbill, and stood there—I have crossed over to tell them to go on—one Friday evening, about two months ago, Mr. Bray's omnibus had been playing round Cornbill; hethen came and wanted to go in between the first and second omnibuses which were standing there—I spoke to Mr. Bray, and said, "Why don't you wait till the first is gone, and then come in third?"—he theb said, "I will be d—d if I don't get them all turned away from the Bank, if it costs me five hundred pounds, and I will line the street with omnibuses. "
PHILIP PARISH ( City police-constable No. 45.) I remember Bray's omnibuses coming to the Bank about Whit-Monday—they principally came in Trevett's time—I did not see what their conduct was at the Bank, as my duty is in the Poultry and Mansion House-street—I remember a
one time, walking up the Poultry with old Mr. Bray, five or six weeks after his omnibuses came—he had spoken about an officer who had been so kind as to assist him in getting up a horse, and had said to him, "Mr. Bray, you are very nervous; get on one side;" and he said he should give him a sovereign, if he might—I said he might—he said he would ask leave, and I belive he did—the sovereign was given, and divided between myself and others—I said to him, "You must be very uncomfortable in driving in opposition in this way: you can't expect these men to give their bread away without trouble"—I told him I thought he would be much more confortable if he had purchased one of their times, as they had purechesed them before—he said, "We should have shown them better"(or something of that)"if Alexander had not deserted me"—(we had before had notice that there were six omnibuses coming down)—he said he should have been very well if Alexander had not put him in the hole, as Alexander had promised to start with him, and then they should have made the Paddington masters look about them.
MR. PAYNE. Q. Have you seen him running in Mr. Bolton's time? A. yes. MR. PAYNE. Q. Have you observed him going in other masters' times? A. Yes.
JURY. Q. Have you observed him going in other masters' times? A. Yes, he has sent out an omnibuses with others—If one goes out, and has not got further then Queen-street, another will follow him—I have seen opposition several times, and if the opposition that has received three minutes comes out, and only gets as far as Bucklersbury, the other that comes behind him goes before him—I have seen one omnibus with the name of "Corbett," following Mr. Bray's the others have gone in their regular turn.
THOMAS CORNEY ESQ . I am Deputy of Broad-street ward. The Bank is situated there—I can hardly say that it is left to me to settle the times of the omnibuses there, but I generally do—the officers of the ward seem to be under my direction—I wrote a circular letter to the omnibus propritores in 1833, in consequence of the great confusion at the Bank, and the Governor of the Bank having written to me that he should put the Bank Act in force; I saw him, and told him I would enter upon it, and make some arrangement, which I did—I was instructed by the police committee to make some arrangements, that times might be set by the proprietors, and kept by the time-keepers—I invited the proprietors to meet me, to make some arrangements which I prescribed; one was, that no longer should any cabs be allowed there, as we found a great many robberies took place, and there were thieves taking advantage and robbing the public—the officers were ordered to remove any persons of that description—a few of the parties met me, but by no means half their number—Mr. Shillibeer was then in opposition with them, not subscribing properly to their funds—I could not get them to terms but by threatening to put the Bank Act in force—they said, if I could get the twenty-nine proprietors to agree to having fifty-nine omnibuses, they would be satisfied, if I pointed out an officer in whom the ward would have confidence—one was appointed, who was to pay no respect to any one, but to summon every person who did not keep his three minutes—that went on for some time, till Mr. Bray began to make a disturbance, and threatened that he would continue it—he said it to me, I belive—I summoned him before the police-committee—he said that he should not belong to their committee—he would run his omnibus and take time, and I had him summoned before the police-committee—he had not had time then, our officers being strictly
bound by the list they had, they could not give him a time, without putting some one out; and in trying to take time, he had caused the obstruction—I could not give him time then; he must have had the concurrence of the committee, as after the arrangement had been made, it was final—after this, Mr. Bray applied to me, and I told him I had ceased then to interfere, the Police-committee of Aldermen not being uniform in their decrees—a worthy Alderman assumed, that this was a monopoly, and therefore he would not sanction any list—I said, "As I formed this list by the wish of a former committee if this court, and you won't sanction it, I will have nothing more to do with it"—before this there had been tolerable order at the Bank—I had no complaints, and if they had not been tolerably well conducted I should have heard—there was good order kept generally, as long as that list was observed—I did not hear of any time being allotted to Mr. Bray, as I ceased after the first list—If he has been admitted, it is in the new list—I know Budd, the time-keeper.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Is this a copy of the letter you sent to the proprietors? A. Yes; it was addressed to all the proprietors—I do not know whether Mr. Bird attended the meeting or no—these are the printed regulations—they were calculated, in my judgment, to benefit the public, and the proprietors also—these regulations were mine; whethere they have acted upon them all, I cannot say.
MR. BODKIN. Q. After the agreements were made, I think some persons were summoned before the Police-committee? A. Yes; for obstructions—the regulations were never binding upon the Police-committee, but the persons were summoned for attempting to break the regulations which the Paddington proprietors had formed—they were convicted in 5 s. or 10 s. each, or imprisoned—the Magistrates of London have convicted persons for causing obstructions, which would not have been caused if the regulations had been attended to.
Cross-examined by MR. SERGEANT BUMPUS. Q. Did Bray call on you, and ask for a time? A. Yes; I cannot say when, I think it was within the last six or eight months.
A JUROR. Q. If I understand you rightly, no person can start an omnibus without entering into this arrangement, the times being filled up? A. I imagine they must make an arrangement to take some time, or else cause an obstruction.
WILLIAM BUDD . I am time-keeper of the omnibuses at the Bank, and was so last Whit-Monday. On that day I observed two of Bray's omnibuses come up—they came in with Mr. Robert Trevett's—I believe it was between ten and eleven o'clock—I was desired to tell them that they were not to take time at the Bank, that was the order of Mr. Corney—I told them so—It was the order that no other omnibuses were to take time, but the 55 which I have on my list—they worked out—they did not come on the stand the first time—they went up Cornhill, and down by the Rank—when the time came for Trevett's to start, I saw Bray's go out with them—one of Bray's conductors said, he would have time, in spite of the cobbler —they seemed to be waiting to go with Mr. Trevett—they continued to come to the Bank, three or four times a day—Bary afterwards applied to the proper authorities, and got his time—he then took his time, following out the omnibuses that he followed in—that was the order of Mr. Theobalds, the marshal—I think that was about a fortnight after—It was after the 13th, but I do not know what day—I cannot call to my recollection after whose omnibus Bray took his time—sometimes he came and took
time, and sometimes he did not—he waited for passengers—that was after he got permission to take time—when he did not take time, he went up Cornhill, round Bank-street, playingmfor passengers, and sometimes he went and waited down Princes-street, before he came to take time—after Bray had got time, there were two omnibuses behind him, but the hind ones are not allowed to solicit passengers, while the first one is there—there were three omnibuses, including Bray's—It is not allowed in the City for the two to apply for passengers, whilst the first is on—that is the agreement of the proprietors—they have no connexion with the City authorities—they have nothing to do with our regulations.
MR. PAYNE. Q. After Bray's omnibuses ceased to run in Trevett's time, who did they run against? A. They ran in Trevett's time for three or four days, and about three days after that they ran in Mr. Bolton's time, for three or four days.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Was it found that these regulations were calculated to promote the convenience of the public? A. Not a doubt of it, Sir, we should do nothing without it—we should be all in conflusion.
COURT. Q. Did Mr. Bray communicate with you on the subject? A. No.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Were you not a police-officer, and sworn in for the purpose of keeping these regulations in force? A. Yes; I was—I was sworn in before Sir Peter Laurie, Lord Mayor—I have the date of the month here—we have had oppositions from Mr. Shillibeer and Newman, and then Mr. Bray came—I have only been there two years—one of Bray's omnibuses ran against Bolton—he commenced by running against Trevett, and then against Bolton, and then against Richard Bird and Johnson—I never saw Mr. Bray till that day—I receive 25 s. a week by the hands of Nathaniel Colaon—he is the relieving time-keeper—he is a peaceofficer—I have observed the omnibuses belonging to the two Bird's, Cowderoy, and Johnson—I have seen Bray's omnibuses drive very steadily from the bank—I am stationed at the Bank—three minutes is the regulation time—they do not stop a longer period.
COURT. Q. Suppose a driver came and wanted to take refreshment, might he not do that before he comes on the stand? A. Yes.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Was not three minutes the time? A. Yes; I have summoned only our own omnibuses, and they were fined 10 s., because they would not go when I have told them.
Q. Mr. Bray ran his omnibuses without regard to time, just as he pleased? A. Yes; there was no regular time—he came when he thought proper—he has run against them all nearly.
Cross-examined by MR. SERGEANT BUMPUS. Q. You are paid by the proprietors, were you named originally by the proprietors? A. I know nothing of the proprietors—I was engaged by Mr. Corney, and paid since by the proprietors, by the hand of Mr. Colson—I am the regular time-keeper at the Bank—Mr. Colson relieves me—when I am not there, he is—when Bray came, on the very first time, on the Monday morning, he tried to take his time at the Bank—he said he should have time—he went up Cornhill—he did not come back in Cowderoy's time that day—I cannot recollect whethere he did the next day—he came once or twice in Bird's time; I think, not so soon as that week; it was about nine days after—he might have come on in their time that week, while I was relieved, for two hours—I remember the "Emperor" and the "Albion" being put on—they came and took time—I have seen the bus with "Sylvester" on it
come about the third day—they name on, and timed themselves off, and tock no account of it—sometime they came on as it suited their purpose; and sometimes they did not—I took no account of it, till I was ordered by Mr. Theobalds—they then took their time—that was Mr. Bray's omnibas—we have a variety of "Emperors"—that was put on againts Bray, with "Sylvester" on it, has come on once I belivee, and taken time, but not more to my knowledge—I believe the "Albion" took time once—they came once, to my knowledge, to the Bank, without taking time—they used to go out with Aaron Bray on other occasions, and I took no account of them, when they worked out odd; that has nothing to do with me.
MR. JONES. Q. You have been asked whether on the first or second or third day, you did not observe Bray's omnibus going in cowderiy's time; did he appear to you to be going more against Travett's time than any other? A. The first two or three days he did—at the time that Bray came on, there were fifty-five omnibuses.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. You have been asked whether you did not observe one omnibus come out, and wait in Princes-street, and of the ownern till Bray came there to run against Trevett, in the first instance? A. Certainly not.
COURT. Q. Was there an omnibus called the "Tally-Ho?" A. That was before the 10th of November—there has been no opposition since, but by Mr. Blower—his is an odd bus —he takes his time regularly at the Bank, following out the buses that he follows in, and that is the regulation in the City, or else we should all be in confusion—I can' tell when Blower had his time—my fellow time-keeper can tell that—I mean the same Robert Belower, who has been called—he does not take regular time—he goes when it suits him.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. That is, when he comes in, you order him to go at his time? A. He does not always come in, and take time—he goes up Bank street, and takes passengers—If a person comes in and takes time I can only insist that he should set off at his regular time—If a man comes in to oppose the regular omnibuses, and remains at the Bank for the time specified he must go—he is liable to be punished under the Bank Act—If he goes when I direct him, he must run against one of the regular omnibuses, which causes so much confusion and obstruction, even on the paverment.
Q. If an opposition omnibus, not coming in and taking the regular times, thinks fit to come for instance at twelve o'clock, must he not beces sarily oppose himself to one of the omnibuses running at that hour? A. Yes; and by that means he creates confusion.
COURT. Q. The times are regulated by the proprietors? A. Yes; there are twenty-two to fill these times—If two more came on it would not only create oppostion, but confusion—If the time was made two minutesit would be no use it would not pay them—we have had very great complaints of so many omnibuses being in the City—If the other omnibuses were as well regulated as ours are, there would not be so much confusion.
JAMES THOMPSON . I am employed by the inspector of stage coaches, at Somerset House.
I take my stand between St. Paul's and Cornhill, at eight o'clock in the morining—my business is to observe the stage coaches that pass, and omnibuses likewise, to cheek the Somerset House returns—I stop till twelve o'clock at night—I have frequently observed Bray's omnibuses for the last three months—I have seen Bray's, Corbett's, and Silvester's coming one against the other—Bray's generally after the other—young Bray has driven his omnibus up, and by placing the pole against the door, prevented passeners getting in—that was a common thing—on one occasion, I observed Raines, one of the conductors, when young Bray derove up get, down when a lady was on the pavement, come and take a child out of her arms, and put it in thje omnibus while the lady was calling on the paverment—when persons have been calling they have been pulled about by different conductors, by Trecett and others, and Bray—they taken hold of people and said, "This is my bus, I am first"—when the people have been going to get into the "Emperor", that being generally first, the passagers would be pulled about by Bray's conductors: and the same conduct was pursued towards the "Albion" by each of Bray's omnibuses—both of the conductors were pugilists, Raines and Mann—sometimes Mann used to drive, and sometimes conduct—I know nothing of him myself.
Cross-examined by MR. SERGEANT BOMPAS. Q. Do you know that Rsines has been a pugilist? A. It has been advartised—I do not know any other conductors pugilists—Murphy has been a pugilist, I have heard—I have heard of Savage—I do not know him—I know the driver of the "Emperor"—I have not been on the box of the "Emperor" when it has been going out of its way after Bray's—I have been on the box twice—I have not followed Bray's omnibus out of the road—I have gone all the way up to Paddington on the box of the "Emperor"—I have gone to Madia-hill upon that very omnibus, when it went out of its regular road to a public-house where they dine—I did not see Bray go there—I saw him before me part of the way—he was there—he dined there—young Bray did—It is about six pr seven weeks sinve I saw the lady with the child—I do not know the name of the public-house—the omnibuses was in the yard—the horses were in the stable—It was near Bray's stable—the "Emperor" did not wait till Bray's went—the "Emperor" started first—I did not see Bray's going just before us.
COURT. Q. What took you there? A. I had been to Mr. Burn, a tailor, at Paddington—I went to Maida-hill for a ride.
MR. JONES. Q. How far was your tailor's from the house where you dined? A. Not a quarter of a mile—they went down I do not know how many back streets—I came back by the same omnibus—I am authourised under the commissioners of the Stamps, by Mr. Lewis Levy—I make my return to him—they go to Somereset-house—my appointment is to inspect the numbers of stage-coaches and omnibuses that pass through cheapside between eight in the morning and twelve at nignt—I am appointed by Mr. Lewis Levy, who is authorizeds by the commissioners of stamps to appoint who he thinks proper.
MR. PAYNE. Q. How long was it after Bray's omnibus ran, that you went to Maida-hill? A. Eight or nine weeks.
JOHN MILLER . I am time-keeper at the wheatsheaf, Edgeware-road, and was so last Whit-Monday. I recollect Bray's omnibus coming out—I cannot say the day—I did not seee Trevatt's omnibus the same day Bray came out—I have repeatedly seen them together—they were trying, of course all to get passengers—first one and them another, and each of them probably in their turns obstrueting the way—I have seen Bray drive his pole against the others, so as to prevent passengers from getting in—he
has waited a quarter of an hour, backing his omnibus against the pavement—I have seen Bray's prevent passengers getting into the others, by pulling close to the omnibus door—I cannot charge my memory whether I have heard Bray's men make use of any particular expressions.
MR. PAYNE. Q. Do you recollect Bray's first starting? A. I recollect their first starting—I have not seen them coming across Paddington Green—I have seen them coming from Bolton's yard, in Church-street—I will not be certain whether I saw any of Bray's drive close to the door of Bolton's omnibus—I do not think I have seen any thing of this—I have seen Bray obstructing any omnibuses that were coming on—he has omnibuses—the pole might probably be over the pavement, which would obstruct passenger from getting in.
NATHANIEL COLSON . I am time-keeper at the New Inn, Edgeware-road. I remember Bray's omnibuses coming out last Whit-Monday—they passed the New Inn to go towards Paddington—I Observed them back—the regular omnibus was standing at the New Inn, that was one of Mr. Trevett's—Bray pulled up ten or twenty yards opposite—not quite so for ward—when Trevett started, Bray started directly and went with him—he appeared to be running to get the roas against Trevett's bus, if possible; they would get the ground where Trevett usually stood—I have seen Bray's people pulling the people in, trying to get there into the bus, from Trevett's or any buses that might be there.
JACOB MILLER ( police-sergeant E 3.) I have been on duty in Oxford-street and Holborn for the last three months—I have observed Bray's omnibus going along—three were running together, Bolton's, Bray's, and I believe Trevett's but I am not certain—I mean the "Albion" and "Emperor"—I have seen persons hail the "Albion" and I have seen Bray's bus draw up to the door of the "Albion, "—I should think that prevented passengers getting in—I have seen Bray's men soliciting passengers from the "Emperor"—there never was a nuisance before Mr. Bray came up—I have threatened to take some of them into custody—I have been threatened to have my head punched by Bray's conductors.
Cross-examined by MR. BOMPAS. Q. Was that when the "Albion" was before and the "Emperor" behind? A. Yes; they were all stopped together—It was Bray's men I was going to take up.
EDWARD TYRRELL . I am a policeman. I have been on duty in Oxford-street and Holborn for the last three years—I have observed Bray's omnibus going along—I have observed Trevett's, but I cannot speak with certainty about them—I have noticed Bray's going along, one before and one behind the other omnibuses—on one occasion I noticed the driver Mann, run against the other omnibus—there seemed to be opposition, one as bad as the other—on two occasions I have summoned Bray's omnibus for misconduct, such as obstructing—he was once fined 40 s.—the other time he had to pay costs—I have observed Bray's men abuse the passengers of the others, using blackguard language to the passengers or the coachman; that was Mann—the abuse was mutual, equal on both sides.
Cross-examined by MR. SERGEANT BOMPAS. Q. At the time you had Bray up were there not four other omnibuses, and did not you have them all up? A. There were six. I only had Bray up, his bus had the name large on it—the other names were small—I did not observe the names on them—I told Mr. Bray if he employed such men to drive, the lives of people were in
danger at all times, because they were improper persons—there might have been some opposition before, but it did not come under my observation—we only had Mr. Bray himself up the second time—he was ordered off by one of our men, and he would not leave.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. You have, under the Police Act, authority to order these men off? A. Certainly; it was for abuse and refusing to go off—It was waiting for one of the others to come up to run agianst it—once there were six together; and when he was summoned on the other occasion he was on a different road, where he had no business to be—the objection was made that he was not plying for hire, but for opposition—before this opposition came on the omnibuses ran perfectly regular—there have been running from Paddington to the Bank as many omnibuses as could be ocnsistent with public convenience and safety.
THOMAS TRINGHAM ( police-constable T 137.) I was on duty at the Wheatsheaf last Whit Monday, and saw Bray's omnibus there, and Trevett's—I have seen Bray, when there have been two standing there according to the regulations, pull in both behind and before the others; and have seen them very frequently crossing each other in going down the Edgeware-road, preventing passengers going in—I have seen them draw the pole of their buses across the other regular bus, and prevent passengers going in on several occasions—I have seen Bray's men soliciting passengers from other omnibuses—I have had occasion to remonstrate with them at the Wheatsheaf—I have told them I must summon them—I have summoned them for furious driving.
MR. PAYNE. Q. Have you seen Bray's omnibus do any thing to Bolton's bus? A. Yes; drive the pole right across the door, and prevent passengers going in.
Cross-examined by MR. WHITEHEAD. Q. What are your instructions? A. To let only two wait there at a time, and those to start every three minutes—I have seen Bray's omnibus come before and behind them—I have seen them stand on the opposite side of the street, at the corner of Cross-street, but very seldom—I have seen the other omnibuses cross Bray's, but I have not seen the others drive up their poles to prevent passengers getting into Bray's—the others were the "Emperor" and "Albion," and sometimes other buses —I summoned Bray once for furious driving, but Bolton was not summoned at that time—Bray was driving furiously down Edgeware-road—the "Albion" was driving against him at that time, and the "Nelson," whcih belongs to Mr. Hill—the other was Bardell's—I summoned those three, and they were all fined 40 s. each.
JOHN FELTHAM . I am a polic-inspector. I am stationed at Paddington—the Edgeware-road comes under my notice—my attention has been directed to the Wheatsheaf—I remember Bray's first starting—I think it was about Whitsuntide, all was then quiet—as soon as Mr. Bray's buses began to run, there was a great opposition—I should say he opposed the whole of them—I cannot say whose time he ran in—I am acquainted with their times—there was the "Emperor" and "Albion," two buses —they went with him or he with them; that caused confusion in the road—I have repeatedly observed Bray's omnibuses drive close up to either of the others—I have seen them come out from Portman-market, perhaps up Church-street, down to the Wheatsheaf—and Bray ran before them or behind them, so as to obstruct people from getting in—I have seen the same at the New Inn, and the half distance, the "Crown"—I have never paid particular attention to
what they said—they have been summoned for furious driving, and wilful misbehaviour in the Edgeware-road.
Cross-examined by MR. BALL. Q. The "Emperor" and "Albion" were with him? A. Yes; they were summoned at the same time, on the 25th of June, and fined.
RICHARD NATHANIEL COLSON . I have had some conversation with Aaron Bray respecting his running his omnibus—he said that unless he could receive 100 l. from the proprietors, he would indict them all for a conspiracy, and ruin them—I remember two of Trevett's men being in custody—Bray said he had been as good as his word—It was not the men he wished to hurt, but their masters, as he could get nothing out of the men.
Cross-examined by MR. SERGEANT BOMPAS. Q. You are one of the time-keepers? A. Yes; I am at all the parts in the course of the day—I pay the other timekeepers—I go from one place to another through the day—I receive the money to pay, from the conductors of the respective omnibuses—I know Mr. Newell—I have not rendered accounts to him latterly—I did some time since—I think the last was six or seven months since—It was the account of what money I received, and what I paid at the time-keepers—I did not account with him as the secretary—I did not know he was secretary—I accounted to him for all the money I received from the conductors, and for all I paid—I never received money from him to make up any deficiency—I have paid trifling sums to him.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Were you subpœnaed as a witness by the prosecutor? A. Yes; I was—I was not called.
MR. SERGEANT BOMPAS. Q. I suppose you are subpœnaed on the other side too? A. I am subpœnaed by the attorney for some of the defendants—I have paid occasional surpluses to Mr. Newell, and I have done so to Mr. Bolton since, from the time I ceased to account with Newell—I received two shillings and sixpence from the conductors for each omnibus—that is supposing they all pay regularly on Saturday evening—sometimes they do not pay regularly—then it is the middle of the next week before I receive their money—I received half-a-crown from each—It amounts to about 6 l. 15 s. a week, and if this has not been all paid to the time-keepers, I pay the overplus to Mr. Bolton—I pay them 6 l. 10 s. a week—I recollect Mr. Bray's omnibus coming on—Johnson has put on an additional omnibus since then, and so has Pearce—I do not recollect that Newell has—Mrs. Watson has—Bolton has put on two omnibuses since, and Trevett, and Cowderoy, and Pearce—the first remark that Bray made, in reference to his omnibuses, was very soon after they started, in Holborn, I think within a week, it was near his own premises, where his omnibuses were in the habit of stopping to change horses—I think there were several people there—there was generally a crowd round his omnibus—the second conversation was on the morning that two of the defendants were taken into custody on this indictment—It was on a Wednesday, the 22nd of June or July—I have a memorandum of the date—Mr. Bray came up to me when the officer signified to the driver and conductor of the omnibus that he wished to take them into custody, that they were his prisoners—he said, "I am very sorry for you, young men; I have no enmity against you, it is not the men, it is the masters I want, I can make nothing of them; the law must take its course;" he concluded by saying," If they will give me 100 l. I will stop all these proceedings, and take away my omnibus "—that is all I can recollect—there were not many days between the first and last
conversations—I cannot say to a day or two—It was not many days—the first conversation was in the street, where his omnibus was stopping, near his own yard in Holborn—the last conversation was when the men were taken—the first conversation was in Holborn also, at the same place—I never saw Bray any where but close to his own premises—his yard is within a hundred yards of the starting place—Mr. Bray was in the habit of going to the place where the omnibus starts from, and he was there—I do not recollect who else was there—there were always people round his omnibus—the "Emperor" and "Albion" were not always before and behind him when they stopped there—I do not recollect whether they were there at the time this conversation took place—there must have been passers-by within ten yards of us—our own omnibuses were there, and the drivers and conductors—I cannot tell what drivers or conductors they were—It was not said loud enough to be heard—It was addressed to me—I should say, it was not loud enough to be heard by people a few yards distant—he was standing by my side—he was waiting there before the defendants came down—there were several people there on this occassion, but not in hearing—he began the first conversation in the usual way, with respect to his omnibuses running—he said he would have his time, and his omnibus should run, in spite of all the opposition of the other omnibuses running against him; and if the proprietors chose to give him 100 l., he would take his omnibus off altogether—that is all he said that I can recollect—this took place at the first conversation, but he had said something to me previous to this.
MR. PAYNE. Q. Did he, in a conversation after the indictment had been found, say he had been as good as his word? A. Yes—I understood that to refer to what he said in the previous conversation—In the first conversation he said he would indict them all for a conspiracy, and ruin them all, unless he received 100 l.
MR. KNOWLES. Q. You have stated that Mr. Pearce has put an omnibus on the road since; does that go at its regular time? A. Yes.
JURY. Q. How many have you now? A. Those that run regularly, and work at three minutes, amount to fifty-five.
MR. KNOWLES. Q. How many had Pearce on the road before Bray? A. Two following each other, and one working at another period—the new one fell into the time of the two—the three did not go exactly in six minutes—the two that followed him and the other worked in twelve minutes—the five worked in twelve minutes—the time before allotted to four—they went at regular times—Mr. Bray did not go at regular times—the new one could not have been in opposition to Bray.
COURT. Q. You have stated three minutes to each one? A. Yes—Pearce and the two before him had twelve minutes for the four; and when he came with the new one, there were five—then, instead of their staying three minutes, they staid two minutes and a fraction—Bray never applied to have any time, only at the Bank—young Bray never asked me to call times for him.
JOHN LEE . I was a driver in the employ of Aaron Bray. I started with one of his omnibuses on Whit-Monday—he told us to do as we thought proper—we started from Holborn to Paddington, to the Wheatsheaf—we then turned round and went to the City with Trevett's omnibuses, because it was a good time—we picked up five or six passengers, which Trevett's buses would have had if we had not—we started first—Trevett's yard is in Praed-street—we followed his omnibuses there—his
omnibuses went into the yard, and then we stopped at the public-house opposite till they came out again—that is out of the line of road from Paddington to the Bank—Mr. Bray has seen us on the line of road, when we were running against Trevett—for some time we ran against Trevett and Bolton—we ran against Trevett six or seven days, and, off and on, against Bolton—Bird's omnibus was on the road—Mr. Bray directed me to run against Bird and Johnson and to leave them till furthur orders, and we continued to do so six or seven days, and I was then taken ill.
MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Did you receive directions from Mr. Bray to run against Trevett? A. No.
Cross-examined by MR. WHITEHEAD. Q. Then your master, the first day, told you he left it to you to drive against who you liked? A. Yes, to do as we thought proper—I never received instructions to drive against Trevett; but I did to run tin Bird and Johnson's time—that was about a fortnight afterwards—the "Albion" and "Emperor" were on the road at that time, I believe—I molested them at times before they started one before and one behind—I endeavoured to get all the passengers I could against them—I have not been driving for Johnson since—I drove for one of his men—I have been driving for Turnbull since.
MR. CLARKSON. A. How long have you been driving for Johnson's men? A. Two or three journeys.
MR. JONES. Q. You say the first day you ran against Trevett? A. Yes; I do not recollect that I told Mr. Bray of it—I do not recollect that he asked me against whom I ran.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. What means did you take to molest the other omnibuses, beside picking up passengers? A. Sometimes I gallopped by them, trotted by them as they stopped to pick up passengers; and when I stopped they came past me—when they have stopped for passengers sometimes I have pulled the pole against the door, and would not let the passengers get in.
JURY. Q. Had you instructions from Mr. Bray to do so? A. No.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Had you instructions from him to do exactly as you liked? A. Yes.
COURT. Q. When was it you began to pull the pole against their omnibus first? A. In two or three days after they began running—I did that to do the best I could for my employer—I pulled the pole first against them—I did so because I thought I was doing the best I could to get passengers.
WILLIAM BIRBECK . I am a time-keeper in Bartholomew-lane. I have seen Mr. Bray's pmnibus twice, and have seen Lee driving it—on the 20th of June I was going through the Poultry—I observed two omnibuses going together—I saw a lady wishing to get into the first—the one behind drove the pole agains the door, so as to prevent the lady getting in—I cannot tell who drove the one behind, but the name of Aaron Bray was on it—the omnibus drove on about three yards—the pole was again driven against the door, and she went away—there is a house in Bargholomew-lane where Trevett's men stop to get drink—I saw one omnibus with the name of "Corbett" on it, and one with "Aaron Bray" on it, stop there when he stopped, and went on when he went on.
Cross-examined by MR. SERGEANT BOMPAS. Q. Were you not one day ordered out by the officers? A. No; I was subpœnaed the day before yesterday—I was here the first day, and then I was subpœnaed—Mr. Goodge acted as time-keeper in the New-road in my absence—I attend there from nine in the morning till nine at night—Corbett's was the "Albion"—I
did not see the "Emperor"—the "Albion" Went first each time, and Bray's after it.
HENRY EDMONDS . I am an oil and colourman, and live in the Edgeware road, and Drury-lane. I remember riding outside the omnibus called the "Emperor," on the Monday after Whit-Monday—It was driven by a man named Sylvester—I got up at Paddington—I observed an omnibus of Aaron Bray's, with its pole close to the door—that was at the end of Farringdon-street—a man of the name of Cuddy Mann was driving—he drove against the hroses, and knocked them down, I walked on some time, and then they got up—I was then frightened at being outside, and went inside—the manner in which the omnibus of Aaron Bray had acted, frightened me—the pole was so close, that the passengers could not get in, and they would not go in either of the buses.
Cross-examined by MR. BALL. Q. Are you a proprietor? A. No; I have got a sum of money lent to two omnibuses in the New-road, called the "Rose," but I am not a proprietor—I have no share in them—one formerly belonged to my father—one belongs to my brother, and the other to Meredith—my name is on it, but it is obliterated, and has never appeared—It was covered before it came out of the painter's loft—I have only an interest in the two "Roses," and one without the name at all, which belongs to Meredith—I do not know whether Meredith is connected with the defendants on the New-road—I am bail for Richard Carpenter, one of the defendants, not for any of the others—I am in the habit of riding from Paddington to my other shop, two or three times a-day—this was the second week that the omnibus started, called "Aaron Bray"—I cannot tell whether the "Albion" was there—I did not see it, but I have seen the omnibus of Braw go to fetch the "Albion" out of the stable—there were several of Mr. Bolton's there at the time I was riding.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Which omnibus did you see go to fetch the "Albion?" A. Two of Aaron's buses, one round at the end, the other square.
Q. How came you to lend money on these omnibuses? A. There was a widow of the same name as myself in difficulty, her husband died—I lend a little money, till it came to 300 l.—I was compelled to take these omnibuses, and a man of the name of Lyons, whom Mr. Norton knows very well, contrived to do the widow out of one of them—I have always understood that Lyons and Nortons were related, and I was afraid to tackle them.
COURT. Q. When did they go to fetch the other omnibus? A. The second Monday after they started.
JOHN BARDELL . I am a veterinary surgeon. I am brother of the two defendants who have been acquitted—I know Aaron Bray—I went to him to inquire why he had put my brothers in the indictment—he told me my brothers must be in indictment, but he should offer no evidence against them, as they were always more friendly to him; but they must be in the indictment, as his attorney had told him so—In consequence of that conversaion, I made an appointment with him to meet him at Mr. Norton's chambers, and I did meet him there on the Sunday—I saw Mr. Norton—I asked Mr. Norton in Mr. Bray's presence, why he advised my two brothers to be put in the indictment—Mr. Norton said, "Bardell, it is hardly fair that you should be here, but I will tell you—Mr. Bray has told me, that he has no evidence to offer against your brothers, but Mr. Bray commissioned your brother to ask the proprietors of the Paddington committee for 100 l. to go off the road;" and Norton said, "If we do not include your
brothers in the indictment, the Paddington proprietors will subpœna them against us, and that would tell very materially against them"—I am positive Mr. Norton said that, and in Aaron Bray's presence; and he said, if the proprietors did not give Mr. Bray 100 l. or take his stock at a valuation, and give him 50 l., that he would suffer to have his arms and legs cut off, if he would not ruin them—I was to tell my brothers, and they would know what they had to depend upon—Mr. Norton said, the people they intended chiefly to offer evidence against, was Mr. Bolton, and Trevett, and they would go in his times—I walked out of the office, and Mr. Bray walked with me—he said "I told you I would be as good as my word, I have met you at Mr. Norton's, and he has told you the reason of your brothers being in the indictment, and unless they give me the 100 l., I shall go on. "
Cross-examined by MR. SERGEANT BOMPAS. Q. You were bail for your brothers? A. Yes; the only reason why I went to Norton's to ask why my brothers were in the indictment was because I had not put in bail at that time—It was last Sunday monthly, I think—I bailed my brothers on the Monday, and this conversation took place on the Sunday—I have given all that took place as near as I can recollect—I did not do to him about the bail—I had no occasion to say a word about it—my bail had not been refused before this Sunday—on the Saturday before I went to Marylebone office Smith my father, with a view, if my brothers wanted bail, to bail them—when I got there, Mr. Norton, and my brothers were in conversation—he said to my brothers, "We will put the thing off till Monday," and my brother said to me, "John, I shall not want bail today, will you bail me on Monday?" and I told him I would—I never want into the office—I was told in the street by my brothers, that it would not be wanted till Monday, and I went on Monday, and it was taken directly—o Sunday, Norton said, "We are willing to take yours and your father's bail on Monday, but it will come to nothing about them"—he said "Your brothers must be in the indictment, and they must bail it, as the rest have done"—they did not mention any names—my father and mine were the names tendered on Saturday, and we were the bail on Monday—I went there in my chaise—I drove young Bray there, and met his father in his chaise there—I waited till he came—old Bray and I went into Mr. Norton's office, and young bray went in the chaise—he came into the office once while we were there—I was there perhaps altogether about an hour and a half, waiting about—old Brag, Mr. Norton, and myself were present when this conversation took place—I do not know whether young Bray was there—I do not remember it—I was to tell my brothers what their decision was, to have the money, or the things sold off—I was to tell the proprietors and my brothers together—we were not in the office an hour and a half—we were waiting some time for Mr. Bray; but it was said, I do not recollect whether at the beginning, middle, or end—I did not tell Mr. Norton I had been to the office the day before, to give bail; but he saw me there, and I saw him—he did not tell me I had not given notice—I did not know it, till I asked the question at Mr. Norton's, whether my brothers were to be indicted' for if they were not indicted, they would not require bail—I did not mention any thing about bail—the conversation commenced with Mr. Norton—Mr. Bray and him had some conversation—Mr. Norton said they would be indicted, and he had a reason for it—I am quite sure he told me that my brothers must be in the indictment—my brother told me he was indicted—I did not know they were indicted—I went to know whether they could not be exempt—Mr. Bray said they
could not; and I went to ascertain the cause why they must be in it; and Mr. Norton said, they must be indicted—old Bray might not have been in the office the whole time—he is troubled with the rheumatism, and in a great deal of pain—he was walking about the room and passage, and sat down to rest himself—I do not know how much of the time he was out of the office—we had some conversation, and I came out; and Mr. Bray told me what Mr. Norton had said—he told me, "John, you see I am as good as my word; I said I would bring you down to Mr. Norton, to know why your brothers were in the indictment, and he has told you; I did commission your brothers to see the Paddington committee; and I must have a hundred pounds and times, or your brothers must be in the indictment, or they would be subpœnaed by the proprietors to give evidence"—Mr. Bray was in and out of the room, when Mr. Norton told me what I have stated—I do not know whether he was near enough to hear—he was walking about the room—I put in bail on Monday—I do not know whether my brothers were members of the committee—I know nothing about it—It is out of my business altogether—I told my brothers this message; and I have told one or two of the proprietors since, when I have seen them—I did not go to Paddington to see them—I had nothing to do with the meeting of the proprietors—I know nothing about it—I have one place in Gloucester-mews, and one in Rathbone-place.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Have you known Aaron Bray some time? A. Yes—he has known me, and has had oppurtunities of seeing the way in which I have conducted myself through life.
JOHN WRIGHT . I am a grocer, and live at the corner of Rathbone-place, Oxford-street. I have been in the habit, before and sinse the 8th of June, of observing the conduct of the omnibuses coming up and down Oxford-street—before Aaron Bray commenced, the omnibuses were perfectly quiet—they would stop at my door and put down, and do every thing quiet—after Bray came, there has been a constant confusion—If an omnibus belonging to one of the proprietors drew up, Bray would drive up very fast behind them, or come in front of them—If an omnibus were to stop, Bray would stop—he would always wait for the other—there appeared no object for him to stop, but he always did stop—when the others have stopped to take up passengers, or set them down, Bray has always stopped, whether he had any one to take up or not—I have observed this conduct for four or five months—I am not interested for either of the proprietors—I have come forward without being subpœnaed.
EDWARD MOORE . I am an undertaker, and live in Rathbone-place, close to Oxford-street, the door is at the corner—I have had my attention called to the omnibuses, going from Paddington to the Bank, up and down Oxford-street, and I have observed the conduct of Mr. Aaron Bray's—the omnibuses were going regularly before he came—after he came, when the omnibuses drew up, Mr. Aaron Bray's drew across them, and prevented the passengers going in them—that has been, if not from the first, very soon after—I observed it in June—I have observed this conduct of Bray's more than twenty times—If Bray has been on the right side, he has tried to come in between them and the pavement—at other times he has gone and drawn across the crossing, to prevent the passengers getting in—I have no interest whatever in the issue of this inquiry—I came without any subpœna.
Cross-examined by MR. WHITEHEAD. Q. How many omnibuses have you
seen with Mr. Bray's? A. I have seen one or two—I did not notice their names—I have seen two frequently, and whenever an omnibus came down the street, Mr. Brays, omnibus would come, and run across it—I have seen the "Emperor"—I cannot swear whether I have seen the "Albion"—I have seen another—I have only observed that when another omnibus has drawn to the side of the street, Mr. Bray has done the same—I have not seen the others do the same to Mr. Bray—I will not swear that I saw Mr. Bray's before the 15th of June.
COURT Q. Did Bray appear to be the aggressor? A. Yes; by constantly crossing over to them.
JOSEPH MASON . I am a chemist, and live at No, 26, Oxford-street, I have had opportunities of observing the conduct of the omnibuses—I have seen Bray's drive as rapid a pace as the others—If one of the others stopped, Bray's would stop also—I have seen that more than once or twice, and I have seen them run the pole very near the other people's door—he has not stopped for a passenger—I have not had that idea—I have not seen any passengers—when the others went on, Bray did so also, and followed them up or down the street, keeping very near indeed to them, as near as they could—I observed that in the first month—I should say Bray's was the aggressor.
Cross-examined by MR. BALL. Q. When you have seen Bray's did you see it with others? A. Yes; the "Emperor"—I cannot state any others, but I have seen it with two or three others.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Was it following them or before them? A. They were altogether.
COURT. Q. Did those other omnibuses appear to you to act towards Bray, as Bray did to them? A. I should say there was no difference in that respect.
JURY. Q. Were you subpœnaed here? A. No; I am here quite disinterestedly—It was intimated to me a friend of the parties that Mr. Holding wished me to come, but I should have come without.
COURT. Q. Who is Mr. Holding? A. He was a very respectable chemist in the same shop—I heave no motive in giving evidence but to clicit the truth.
JOHN WILLIS . I am a hosier, and live at No. 371, Oxford-street. I have had opportunities of observing the omnibuses that drive up and down from paddington to the Bank for the last five years—those with "Aaron Bray" on them have been conducted in the most did orderly way imaginable—previous to the omnibuses of Aaron Bray coming on the road, they were conducted as orderly as it was possible for vehicles of that description to be; so much so, that I allowed them, two years ago, to change horses at my house—since Aaron Bray began running up the street, to my coat, I have found that the disorder has been so great that ladies could not come in their carriages to my shop—they were afraid—I have seen the other omnibuses stop to take up passengers on several occasions—I have seen Bray's omnibuses pull up with the pole against the door of the other omnibuses, to prevent a passenger from getting in, in more instances than one—from what I have seen, Bray was decidedly the aggressor, because I considered he was in opposition to the others—before Bray came, the road was orderly—they were going quitely about their business.
Cross-examined by MR. SERGEANT BOMPAS. Q. When this took place, was the "Emperor" the one before or behind it? A. That I could not say; but I have noticed Mr. Bray's being a blue omnibus.
COURT. Q. During the time you have paid attention to these omnibuses, upon your oath, have you seen any difference in the conduct of the
other omnibuses towards Bray's, or have been nearly the same? A. I have noticed that when another omnibus has been going down the street quietly, Mr. Bray's has driven up as near to them as possible—I have not seen the others serve Bray so.
JAMES GRAPE . I am a wine and spirit merchant, and live at No. 412, Oxford-street, I have observed the omnibuses drive up and down from Paddington to the Bank—I have had frequent opportunities of observing the conduct of the drivers—the conducting of the omnibuses before Bray's came on, was upon a very excellent principle, and since that, they have been a great annoyance to the passengers—I have had occasion to ride down to the City, having a nervous debility, and have been annoyed by Mr. Aaron Bray's omnibus coming close to the one I wished to get into—I think that Bray's omnibuses were the aggressors.
Cross-examined by MR. WHITEHEAD. Q. Why do you think they were the aggressors? A. Because I have seen the others drive to get a-bead get ahead of the others—I have never seen the others drive to get a-head of them. not when I have been fiding in them—I have seen the omnibuses driving past each other—I have seen two or three omnibuses with Bray's—I think I can swear I have not seen so many as five—I will go as far as two or three—I will not go farther—I have not noticed the names of the omnibuses—there are so many passing and re passing; but I noticed Bray's, because there are only two of them, and I suppose there are thirty others—Bray's always seemed to be driving up to the spot where I live—they came on the road, in my opinion, to oppose others—I have noticed them more particularly—they did not drive up to my house, but nearly so; within twenty yards or so—they all stop there.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. When you have seen them, was the conduct you complain of, the conduct of Bray's omnibuses? A. Yes.
JAMES KENDALL . I am a victualler, and live in Rathbone-place. I have noticed the omnibuses that go along Oxford-street repeatedly—I have observed Bray's omnibuses—when I have been riding in another, they have repeatedly crossed the front of the bus that I was riding on, and pulled up by the side of it or behind it; that has caused a great annoyance, so far, that I felt inclined to get off the one I was on—our omnibus went on the same as usual—I felt annoyed by those buses constantly drawing up infront of the bus I was on—I do not know that the rest of the passengers did—Bray's pulled up in the usual way of pulling up—I did not observe that they did it to take up a passenger—I cannot say what reason they had for pulling up, but they did pull up—I did not see them take up passengers when they pulled up.
---- MUNDAY. I live at No. 20, Harrow-road. I am conductor to Mr. Richard Bird, and have been so seven or eight months—I recollect the time of Bray's omnibus coming on—It was in the Whitsun-week—I have observed the conduct of Bray's to my master, because he was driver to the one that I was conductor to—they have told him they would not leave him—they meant to stick to his times.
Cross-examined by MR. WHITEHEAD. Q. What had he said to them before? A. My master said to young Bray, "What do you come in my
time for? it is very hard to come against a poor man getting his living"—he made answer, "We mean to stick to you, we fancy you. "
T. Bolton and R. Trevett — Guilty — Fined 100 l. each.
R. Bird, T. Bird, W. Cowderoy, J. Hill, J. Johnson, and J. Parkes — Guilty — Fined 50 l. each.
R. Carpenter, J. Newell, S. Pearce, and P. Sellick — Guilty — Fined 30 l. each.
T. Corbett, E. Sylvester, — Guilty — Fined 20 l. each.
G. Dodd, W. Gulliver, J. Gough, T. Trevett, J. Sylvester, and J. Smith — Guilty — Fined 5 l. each.
The Jury recommended to mercy those defendants whom the Court thought deserving of it.
